snapback4/reports/20260929-sl-suite-under-load/guide-witness.txt:389:E_WITNESS_PROOF means recorded bytes or native startup identity did not match.
snapback4/reports/20260930-na-native-acquisition/round1/affected-sdk.log:559:    "Replace alice/heldNotes with that persona/query; SB4_URL is dev's URL, SB4_PROOF a new directory:\n" +
snapback4/reports/20260930-na-native-acquisition/round1/affected-sdk.log:562:    'snapback4 witness acquire --as alice --url "$SB4_URL" --query heldNotes --restart --artifacts "$SB4_PROOF" --json\n' +
snapback4/reports/20260930-na-native-acquisition/round1/affected-sdk.log:596:    actual: '//! The bounds card. Quarry examples are compiled by tests/guide.rs.\n// HV web figures: reports/20260929-hv-heavy-acquisition/REPORT.md, five-run table.\npub(super) const CARD: &str = r##"# Bounds and the meter\n\nThe meter limits the work each read, mutation and job transaction can do.\nDeclare product limits; the engine chooses execution bounds within those ceilings.\n\n```quarry\ntable scalePosts:\n  body: text <=500\n  public\n  online only\nquery scalePage(c: cursor ?):\n  return scalePosts first 20 by .id after c\nquery scalePopulation():\n  return count(scalePosts)\n```\n\nWrite product limits: a page size, a text length, an asset size or a real per-group cap.\nDo not choose an index, a merge width or a job transaction size. Predicate reads and views choose their plans; top-level job `for`\nloops choose their chunks. Removing a written number never removes the meter.\n\nA query\'s `for row in t where .owner = viewer:` and a merge lane\'s source may omit `first N`\nwhen the read rule is uniformly decided by that prefix; they read ascending\nuntil the prefix ends or the meter cuts, and `check --cost` prints `rows ≤ meter`\nand `lanes ≤ meter` when no fitting schema cap supplies a static bound.\nA loop the meter cuts has no continuation; a total over a group that can outgrow one read is a maintained `view`.\nA returned or bound page instead needs a schema cap or an explicit page bound.\nJobs may iterate uncapped domains under the chunk\'s meter. Top-level job\n`for` sweeps shrink their transaction size after a meter refusal. Mutation loops\nstill need a schema-capped group that fits the examined ceiling. Direct\n`count(domain)`, `sum(domain, .column)` and `exists domain` stream under the\nmeter without retaining the page. Schema-capped query `count` and `sum` with\nnon-uniform read rules keep their bounded page execution and coverage contract.\nMutations retain their uniform-read and plain-prefix admission rules; a cut aborts the transaction, never commits a\npartial total. A streaming query total with a meter cut or unknown device\ncoverage leaves the client read `loading`, never a partial count or sum.\nUnknown earlier reads also make a streaming scalar unavailable, including\ninputs saved in locals or used as domain bounds; a fallback cannot establish\ncoverage.\n\n## Allocation and completeness\n\nHeap is a cumulative allocation and binding budget. Streaming aggregates\nrelease each transient row and its key scratch; one cursor allowance remains.\nInner job domains decode one prepaid row at a time and retain each selected\nrow under the heap meter before fetching another. Once decoded, the row\'s\nretained allowance replaces its decode scratch. Retained rows, their vector\nand the live cursor stay charged; domains do not retain unused result keys.\nConstructed values,\nconcatenation and other allocating builtins, local bindings, and owned\ncopies count. Inspecting a held value\'s length or sum borrows it; row and\nmap clones share their immutable image. A heap cut stops a query immediately\nwith no available value or continuation, since an interrupted scalar or shape\nhas no valid partial answer. The client read is `loading`; narrow the query or\nuse a maintained view for a total. Its cut site remains internal;\nthe meter is available only through dev\'s default read inspection (CLIENT-AND-OPERATIONS.md §4).\nAn unfinished maintained lookup or live aggregate uses the same unavailable\nquery result, preserving zero only for a completed missing-group lookup.\nA mutation rolls back; Operations below describes the refusal.\nA page whose read rule the prefix does not decide (the deciding fields include a\ncolumn the scan does not pin) is never `complete`: the server cannot tell\n"no more" from "more you may not see". Pin the deciding columns in the predicate, or read the page on the device (`guide sync`), where the\npartition holds exactly what the viewer may read. `check` says so\n(`N_NEVER_COMPLETE`), as it notes a query page returned without a cursor\n(`N_NO_CURSOR`), a synced table whose groups can grow with no horizon\n(`N_NO_HORIZON`), and a merge past 50 lanes (`N_WIDE_MERGE`).\n`complete` means the query answered its declared request within known coverage and execution limits.\nFilling `first N` or `last N` is complete, including a composed preview.\nA cursorless page cannot continue; declare `c: cursor ?` and use `after c`\nif the screen pages. Composed fields and pages covering a fitting group cap\nomit `N_NO_CURSOR`.\n\n## Operations\n\nNothing is priced at check. As a program runs, the meter counts examined\nrows, index entries, rule probes, steps, heap bytes, output bytes and rows\nwritten against ceilings: 8,192 rows examined, 16,384 index entries, 8,192\nrule probes, 1,000,000 steps, 16 MiB heap, 1 MiB output, 1,024 rows written\nper mutation; a job\'s chunk may examine 16,384 rows and write 8,192. A\nquery past a ceiling returns what it found with `complete: false`; a\nmutation aborts with E_BOUND naming the meter, the ceiling and the site\n(never the count: it would measure rows the viewer may not read).\nRead a smaller page, narrow its predicate, or move the sweep into a job.\nAuthorization-dependent scans prepay a fixed logical allowance before row\ndiscovery. Hidden candidates do not consume the caller\'s remaining allowance;\nreadable rows, filters and output still do. These logical meters exclude private\nscan work: authorization candidates share one physical limit of 8,192\nper request, including nested witnesses and maintained-source walks. A row behind at least 8,192 such candidates is unreachable by paging\nin that direction; an empty page has no cursor into the hidden range. Expiring\ncap checks draw from that same allowance; any authorization that cannot be\nestablished within it returns generic E_RULE without identifying a cause.\nThe device\'s prediction-work budget can instead queue an operation.\n`snapback4 check --cost` prints each site\'s structural class;\n`snapback4 test` prints the numbers on your data and names query/persona reads\npast half any ceiling without a cut: at most ten lines and a count of the rest.\nLoopback `dev` logs actual meter and word cuts with the operation, principal,\nsite, counters and `snapback4 why <op>`. Repeats are suppressed per operation\nand site (at most two lines per minute); the next emitted line includes the\nsuppressed count. `serve` neither logs these costs nor returns read meters.\nA loop is bounded by a page\'s `first N`, a schema-capped prefix, a query\'s meter-bounded prefix, a list\'s `<=N`, `range`\'s int expressions, `walk`\'s depth,\nor `tokens`\' 64. An accumulated list is bounded by the heap meter and its\niterations by the step meter. `while` stays refused.\n`range(a, b)` evaluates int expressions once and iterates under the step meter,\nwithout materializing the range. `check --cost` prints `steps ≤ meter`, a\nfiltered scan as `filter over ≤ examined ceiling`, its reads as `probe × examined`,\nand a seek as a prefix. Sorting or grouping only processes that page.\n`sha256(text)` charges the input\'s UTF-8 byte length plus one to steps before\nhashing, then 64 heap bytes for its fixed lowercase hexadecimal result. A step\ncut refuses a scalar query with E_BOUND; a cut on that result allocation leaves\nthe client read `loading`, as other query heap cuts do. A mutation rolls\nback on either cut.\n\n### Prove the heaviest account\n\nProve the envelope\'s heaviest account: 500 follows and 1,000 conversations need acquisition/restart proof.\nReplace alice/heldNotes with that persona/query; SB4_URL is dev\'s URL, SB4_PROOF a new directory:\n\n```sh\nsnapback4 witness acquire --as alice --url "$SB4_URL" --query heldNotes --restart --artifacts "$SB4_PROOF" --json\n```\n\nFor serve or a real account, use `--as <principal> --token <bearer>` (or `--token=<bearer>`). Optional `--args \'{"c":null}\'`\nsupplies query arguments; without `--query` the witness selects the first zero-argument device query. `--runs 3` uses three\nfresh stores. Default URL is http://127.0.0.1:4400; `--timeout 120` bounds each run. The command writes acquire.json even on\nfailure; --json prints it, otherwise one summary per run. Receipt excerpt (counts depend on your fixture):\n\n```json\n{"passed":true,"runs":[{"coverage":{"groups":1,"rows":2},"restart":{"retained":true,"downloadedRows":0}}]}\n```\n\nNode sends one JSON line per call to the SQLite `snapback4 device` subprocess. Expo links the same crate in-process; these are\nnot Expo/iOS or physical-phone timings. `acquiredMs` ends at SDK acquired; `firstLocalMs` ends at the subsequent complete local\nquery. Both start at fresh startup, including identity checks. Restart firstLocalMs includes cold offline reopen;\n`restart.coverageVerifiedMs` separately times coverage scan/comparison. Retained means equal store/generation/watermark,\nper-group counts/coverage and table rows/bytes. Reconnect must finish a validated `more: false` stream at the applied server\nposition, with no row events or failed attempts. Even an empty snapshot at watermark zero needs catch-up. Keep the fixture\nstable: writes/expiry invalidate comparison. OS caches are warm; app UI and media caches are excluded. Verification scans follow\nthe timed answer; background polling is suppressed. Groups are table/group pairs with rows/coverage, plus implicit\npublic/principal groups. groupsByTable and residency.tables hash names with SHA-256 of UTF-8 JSON.stringify(["witness-table",\nname]); use name = "messages" for conversation groups. residency.totalBytes is SQLite pages, excluding WAL/SHM and RSS; table\nbytes are row payloads. Receipt format 2 retains local timings/counts and computed state/position digests; server text, codes,\nnumeric metadata and Server-Timing are dropped. Validated codes select argument-rejection, query-refusal or incomplete-coverage\ndiagnostics: check --args types, --query requirements or held coverage respectively. Failed receipts retain caller query/args.\nAutomatic queries get a generated label; explicit query/args/principal echo only caller input.\n\nHV web medians (eb1000, five r'... 17885 more characters,

# LLP 3000.000.000: Retained storage and hot release activation

**Type:** RFC
**Status:** Implemented
**Systems:** Schema evolution, Storage, CLI, Deployment, Replica generations
**Author:** Codex for Charlie Cheever (Workstream ROLL)
**Date:** 2026-10-12
**Revised:** 2026-10-13 (§10: REL2 wave-10 release lifecycle); 2026-10-13 (MACFIX: explicit pre-retention compatibility boundary and canonical deployment/worker paths); 2026-10-12 (§5.1: path-independent control socket; implemented; dual-family confirmation, captured lifetimes, native generations, hot activation and complete qualification)
**Related:** [LLP 3000](./3000-snapback4.explainer.md), [LLP 3000.000](./3000.000-atomic-schema-and-release-activation.research.md), [LLP 3000.007](./3000.007-relaxed-quarry-and-explicit-residency.rfc.md), [LLP 3000.019](./3000.019-what-the-builder-still-wrote.rfc.md)

## 1. Commission and scope

Charlie approved the ideal end-game release lifecycle and commissioned implementation on 2026-10-12. Implementer: this ROLL session, beginning that date. Wave 8 DT1 found additive rollback deletes new storage in all 50 day-two lanes; DT1a found unnamed enum conflicts; DT10 found deployment requires stopping serve. This supersedes the release-only removal semantics in LLP 3000.000's implementation follow-up. Development and device replacement keep their existing projection/reset semantics. No new publication authority or gate is introduced.

## 2. Retain by default

Every release activation (deploy and rollback) retains removed durable application tables, columns and index definitions in SQLite-owned dormant storage within the same activation transaction. The active r/i image remains an exact projection of the active schema. Dormant data is outside that namespace and unavailable to programs, rules, references, queries, sync, SQL application projections and clients. The Quarry/IR binder admits only declared application table names; it has no general application SQL evaluator. Dormant internal names are not added to Schema, and schema validation forbids declarations in the reserved `_dormant_` and `_release_` namespaces. Inspection `snapback4 sql` is owner-only and may enumerate it. A future application SQL adapter must keep its declared authorized input allow-list. Asset reads must resolve the home table, column and row in the active schema/r image and check the current read rule; a dormant home refuses E_ASSET_UNKNOWN even when a caller knows its id. Test the reserved-name/program boundary and direct fetch by id. Reintroducing a dormant name deliberately reactivates its data, after full validation under the candidate schema. A deployment that reuses any dormant name refuses unless --restore-dormant explicitly selects restoration; its refusal lists retained items/counts/samples and the alternatives `deploy <dir> --restore-dormant` or `prune <dir> --allow-destructive` before fresh creation. Rollback's selection of an immutable older release explicitly selects restoration; `rollback <dir> --to N` also selects an immutable release for deliberate roll forward. To convert durable to ephemeral, deploy a release without that table, prune it explicitly, then deploy the ephemeral definition. This applies to deployments as well as rollback; a rename remains two names, without an inferred data move.

Inventory and audit entries carry a stable typed key (`table:name`, `column:table.name`, or `index:table.name`); pagination uses the returned key, not a bare path that could collide between columns and indexes. The SQLite format is `_dormant_tables(table_name, definition)` for whole-table Table definitions, `_dormant_rows(t,id,data)` for their stored images, `_dormant_columns(table_name,column_name,definition)` for retired column type/default/ownership, `_dormant_values(t,id,column_name,data)` for their cells, `_dormant_indexes(table_name,index_name,definition)` for index definitions and `_dormant_refs(t,id,column_name,parent_t,parent_id,invalid)` for deletion evidence with a parent lookup index. Catalog numbers are reserved monotonically and reused for the same physical name, but dormant rows are never kept in r under a hidden catalog number. These tables are created only during successful release activation; legacy deployments start empty and cannot recover data previously deleted by older binaries. A successful release activation writes the release-only format marker `version=snapback4-release-store-3`. New Store open/version checks accept this, the prior snapback4-release-store-2 and the legacy snapback4-store-1; old binaries reject it with E_STORE rather than opening it. Development and device stores retain snapback4-store-1 and their formats are unchanged. The format marker rolls back with failed activation. On restore, omitted cells from whole-table images move to dormant column cells before projection, merging previous retired cells; full-table entries are consumed only on commit.

The compatibility boundary is the store format, independently of schema
capabilities. Published 0.2.33 refuses retained release-store versions 2 and 3
with `E_STORE: store version <version> is not snapback4-store-1; this data
directory belongs to another Snapback` (the read-only doctor form omits
the final data-directory explanation), including capability-free releases.
That refusal must preserve committed state and never interpret dormant data
under the old layout. Capability-free backend/schema bytes and unchanged
store-1 development/device fixtures retain their separate compatibility
controls (LLP 3000.022 §14.3); they do not establish old-server readability of
new release stores. The Darwin publication fence checks both boundaries.
The MACFIX compatibility/path amendment passed independent final
[Muse](./reviews/3000.000.000-macfix-r3.muse.md) and
[Codex](./reviews/3000.000.000-macfix-r3.codex.md) reviews at e9150bd171.
The required Darwin schema and worker files passed alone with default TMPDIR
(10 and 82 tests); whole-publication qualification is a separate claim.

Dormant whole tables store their row images; dormant columns store values keyed by table/column/row id and their definitions. Capture interpreted literal defaults and optional nulls when a column becomes dormant, preserving the value existing rows actually saw. Keep index definitions as history; preserve their data logically and rebuild physical keys on reactivation from current rows. Dormant indexes are never consulted for active writes, uniqueness or caps. Thus a new duplicate under the older release can precisely refuse forward activation. Derived rows are caches and are rebuilt, not authoritative history. Retention must precede existing derived-state retirement. An unsupported durable-to-ephemeral conversion refuses rather than silently discarding stored data.

Partial restoration consumes only candidate-visible cells: B's T(a,b) → absent T → C's T(a) restores a and moves b to dormant cells, with b's definition, then B restores b. For an identical (t,id,column) cell key, the newly retired active value wins: it is the most recent authoritative value. Preserve other dormant keys. Use transactional upsert and test retire → restore → update → retire → restore. Keep zero-row table/column/index definitions too. Whole-table restoration first restores its stored image, splits omitted cells into dormancy, then projects the active image. Consumption shares commit with validation. Refuse application-table to derived-table ownership changes, or authoritative-column to maintained/captured ownership changes, including when the application table/column was dormant. Derived caches never overwrite retained authoritative data. The refusal names the incompatible owner and directs a deliberate rename or prune while dormant.

At retirement record reference edges for dormant rows/cells (including optional/list references), bound to their current parent lifetime. An active delete transaction marks matching dormant edges invalid before the id can be reused, even for identical replacement values. Only a move into dormant storage or replacement of a derived cache is exempt from deletion invalidation. Actual authoritative-row deletion always invalidates matching dormant edges, including expiry during activation; failed activation rolls the invalidation back. Restoration refuses invalidated edges or missing parents with table/column/parent ids, counts and samples; it cannot silently recapture another parent's audience. For read-through rows, compare preserved captured audience and identity fields to the candidate parent before existing recapture work. A changed audience refuses restoration; inherited deadlines keep their original capture. Newly expired rows remain logically invisible and may be swept only under the reactivated schema. Test deletion/reuse, identical replacement, changed audience, nested references and expiry. This adds no device incarnation format.

Restoration, candidate validation, derived rebuild, catalog/generation/programs and current release commit together. Any error rolls all of them back. Retained values overwrite no explicit candidate writes: restoration precedes activation builders. Incompatible name reuse must pass candidate types and constraints; no flag bypasses validation.

## 3. Older writes and incompatible values

Updating an active row while its newer columns are dormant preserves those dormant values. Deleting that row deletes its dormant column values in the same transaction: forward never resurrects a deleted row, including when its id is reused. New rows written under the older schema have no dormant value. On forward they receive the candidate literal default or optional null; required columns without such a default refuse with exact missing-row counts and up to five ids. Repair by deploying a bridge schema with that column optional or a literal default using --restore-dormant, fill it through a bridge mutation, then deploy the desired required schema. Alternatively explicitly delete the named rows through the current release and retry. Never tell the older schema to write a field it lacks. Contextual now/viewer defaults do not invent historical values. Existing required-column backfill rules still apply.

Release activations retain incompatible existing values per row rather than refusing a type narrowing. Section 9 specifies the visibility, reference and restoration rules. Development adoption keeps its checked narrowing behavior. Missing newly required fields still need the bridge described above; retention applies to present historical values, not absent backfill. No implicit coercion or fallback is introduced.

For every data-dependent refusal, aggregate count and bounded samples (five distinct values/keys, 256 UTF-8 bytes each, plus truncation markers) at the failed column/index/constraint, without unbounded row retention. Include actual conflicting values for column failures, index name and conflicting keys for unique failures, and actionable command or schema repair. Detailed values are privileged operator diagnostics only, never ordinary application refusal payloads, query meter diagnostics or sync refusals. No claim that a green read-only preview freezes later writes: adoption rechecks under its transaction.

## 4. Explicit pruning and audit

snapback4 prune <dir> runs through the serving owner when live, otherwise the offline owner. It lists dormant tables, columns and indexes with row counts, logical serialized dormant bytes (including retained asset bytes separately) and at most five ids; it changes nothing without --allow-destructive. With that flag it permanently deletes exactly the currently dormant storage in one writer transaction, records a durable prune event, and leaves active rows/programs untouched. It releases dormant asset homes for the ordinary collector. Deploy/rollback --allow-destructive do not prune; direct users to the separate command. Prune intentionally deletes all dormant items as one reviewed storage boundary; per-item dependency-aware pruning is deferred because partial pruning can strand dormant references and asset homes. There is no implicit size or age collector: previews make storage growth visible without inventing a data-loss policy. After prune, reintroducing the schema gets empty tables and normal required-column/default behavior. Pruning is an irreversible operator action; the command output names every item dropped.

Release activation events live in the database transaction with activation revision, operation, release/previous/generation, timestamp and retained/restored summary. Prune records its item summary in that transaction. Existing releases.jsonl and current remain checked convenience copies; post-commit filesystem errors explicitly say activation succeeded. History is never edited or rewound. Rollback picks an earlier immutable source, not a historical data snapshot.

Dormant bytes have no automatic age-based deletion: retention lasts until explicit prune, and prune preview exposes populations so operators control disk use. Dormant asset homes remain live in the asset ledger but are unreadable because the active schema has no home. Active-row deletion also retires any associated dormant asset home. Dormant rows do not run old expiry or jobs while dormant; candidate liveness applies again upon reactivation. Explicit application deletion and ordinary active expiry still mean deletion, not an undo archive.

## 5. Hot activation and ownership

serve owns the database sidecar for its lifetime and publishes a Unix-domain release-control socket in the private per-user runtime directory (§5.1; directory 0700, socket 0600). Check peer credentials on both ends (Linux SO_PEERCRED, Darwin getpeereid), requiring the effective UID. Only the host filesystem owner has this capability; it is not an HTTP door, browser origin, bearer service seat or remote endpoint. Check filesystem objects before replacing a stale socket; never follow symlinks or replace a regular file. Use `.release-command` (a hardened private regular file, no-follow, single link) for the command flock and the deterministic runtime address (§5.1) for the control socket. lstat socket entries; only a same-owner socket may be removed while holding the exclusive store sidecar. Bound command JSON to 4096 bytes and response JSON to 64 KiB, with a 5-second input timeout and 120-second command response timeout. The trusted same-UID operator can directly edit source/database; malicious same-UID edits are outside the threat model, but ordinary source races are caught by a candidate content digest over source/native/worker files in the request and verified by the server. Numeric release id is the only source selector; never a caller path. Commands hold a separate private command flock while allocating/staging immutable release source. They acquire the store owner when stopped; E_STORE_BUSY with a running serve uses the local socket instead of opening another writer. Dev and an older/unreachable server continue to refuse with an exact stop/retry command.

snapback4 releases <dir> reads authoritative current release, activation revision, generation and durable events without adopting source, including while serve is live. This is the recovery command for an ambiguous result. A bounded command message identifies the owned database inode/canonical identity, candidate release id, expected current release and monotone activation revision, operation and explicit flags. The server compiles only its own immutable release directory, prepares native module/worker prerequisites before commit, then holds its existing engine writer guard for activation and in-memory publication. The server verifies both expected current release and activation revision inside the activation transaction. Increment activation revision on every successful release activation, including an equal-hash source change; A→B→A cannot satisfy an old command. Concurrent commands serialize on the command flock; out-of-band stale commands fail compare-and-swap. No request may choose arbitrary filesystem source. Compilation/validation failure leaves the previous engine, bound schema, native module, worker revision and reader factory unchanged.

A query already running in a pinned SQLite read transaction finishes on its old snapshot and reports that generation. A writer already executing finishes before activation obtains its engine guard. Queued HTTP operations are admitted under one release request guard covering signature lookup, argument decoding and execution. Activation acquires its exclusive side before taking the engine guard; admitted operations finish before activation, and later operations dispatch under the new generation. Internal detached readers still fence obsolete identities with E_GENERATION. Fresh requests after publication use the new engine; a stale pooled reader not yet pinned retries through the replacement factory. Prepare a runtime bundle containing bound programs, precompiled native module/metadata, worker root and validated named declarations; prepare the reader factory from the post-activation engine while its writer guard blocks new writers (reader opening failure after commit clears the old factory and uses existing lazy retry, never aborts a committed activation or advertises the old backend). Swap bound/generation/native under the common bound guard in the existing /schema guard order, retire pooled readers, rebind ephemeral state, invalidate change answers, wake sync/polls and update worker revision while holding the writer guard. The supervisor explicitly switches its immutable release project root and named declarations on revision, including addition/removal/replacement; externally supplied --worker stays an operator-owned entry. Worker preparation captures an immutable (root, entry, declarations/environment, supervisor revision) tuple. Under the engine guard it compares that captured revision to the current worker revision before minting credentials; stale preparation is discarded and rediscovered. Never relabel an old prepared source with the latest revision. Credentials bind that checked revision so later activation fences them even when spawn finishes later. Workers restart using the candidate packaged source and native continuation hashes keep the existing conservative parking policy. Long validation can pause writes; hot means no process restart, not an unbounded zero-pause latency promise.

The server continues an admitted activation even if the client hits its 120-second response timeout or closes the socket; failure to write the reply is not permission to roll back or exit the server. The database is authoritative at crash or lost socket reply. Timeout/EOF after sending a command reports an ambiguous result and instructs inspection of current and durable history; never silently resend as a new release or claim failure left the old release active. Before activation fsync required immutable source/native/worker files and their directories, then the published releases directory entry. The supported guarantee covers process crashes and filesystem/SQLite durability on hosts honoring fsync; no guarantee covers failing storage hardware. Restart attaches the database-selected release even if current's convenience copy is stale. After database commit, publication is infallible in-memory assignments and committed ephemeral rebind; fallible native compilation, declaration/environment/import validation happens before commit. Worker process spawn remains fallible after commit: failure reports committed activation with degraded worker availability and retries; it cannot undo a release or external effects. Filesystem-copy failures report the committed result.

### 5.1 Path-independent control address

Amendment commissioned and implemented by Codex for Charlie on 2026-10-12,
for the 0.4.24 macOS publication blocker. Darwin's Unix socket path limit
is 104 bytes; the previous deployment-local name failed under ordinary
`/var/folders/.../T/` fixtures. Linux's descriptor-address workaround did not
solve Darwin or provide one portable transport.

Use `/tmp/snapback4-<effective-uid>/<sha256>.sock` on both hosts. The hash is
SHA-256 over the raw bytes of the canonical **deployment directory**, binding
the socket to immutable release source. Aliases that canonicalize to the same path resolve
to one address; two deployments pointing to one external database retain
separate addresses, so only the serving deployment can submit its releases.
At deploy and serve startup, create a missing deployment privately, resolve
its canonical directory once, then use that directory for ownership and all
release paths. Serve also resolves the selected release root before constructing
the project. Worker cwd and `SNAPBACK_PROJECT_DIR` therefore report the same
canonical active release path, including Darwin's `/var` → `/private/var` and
explicit deployment symlinks. An existing deployment alias resolves before
private-directory validation; no symlink is installed inside private storage.
Use the same canonical path spelling on case/normalization-insensitive hosts;
byte-distinct canonical spellings produce distinct addresses. The request
envelope still binds the canonical database path, device and inode.
The longest address is 95 bytes (ten decimal UID digits), below both limits.
Do not use TMPDIR or XDG_RUNTIME_DIR: these may be long or differ between CLI
invocations. This deterministic address needs no durable locator file; deploy
and rollback derive exactly the same address from durable deployment location.
A moved deployment must stop and restart serve, as moving its immutable source
already requires. Socket entries are ephemeral, never backup authority.

Create the single runtime directory atomically as 0700. Open with O_DIRECTORY
and O_NOFOLLOW, inspect the opened metadata, and refuse a different UID, a
non-directory/symlink or any mode other than 0700 (including special bits).
Never repair or chmod an existing directory. `/tmp` is the platform's shared
sticky temporary root (Darwin may alias it to `/private/tmp`). An untrusted
user may pre-create a conflicting name and cause refusal, but gains no access
and causes no modification of their entry. Malicious same-UID filesystem edits
remain outside §5's threat model.

Bind only while holding the exclusive database sidecar. An existing entry
must be a same-owner 0600 socket. Probe it: a live listener refuses without
unlinking; only connection-refused/not-found permits stale replacement. Any
other error refuses. Preserve the inode check around client connect, same-UID
peer checks on both ends, envelope identity, command flock and revision CAS.
Establish inode-based cleanup custody immediately after bind, before chmod or
nonblocking setup; Drop removes only that owned socket inode. Graceful shutdown
joins control before releasing store ownership. Fatal exit after permission setup leaves a stale
socket for the next exclusive owner. A crash between bind and chmod may leave
a socket with the launching umask's mode; if it is not exactly 0600, recovery
refuses and the operator must remove that stale entry. Do not change the
process-global umask in a multithreaded host to hide this fail-closed window. Keep the shared runtime directory on exit:
removing it would race other deployments, and it contains no durable state.

Acceptance: default-TMPDIR project paths exceeding 108 bytes on Linux and
Darwin start serve, execute hot deploy and rollback without restarting, refuse
a second host, clean up on SIGTERM, and restart after SIGKILL. Unit regressions
cover stale/live socket entries, file/symlink and 0600 socket refusals, directory
permissions/type/UID checks, canonical aliases, independent deployments, and
preserving replaced entries during cleanup. SIGTERM/SIGINT/SIGHUP also drain
an incomplete control request before process exit; join the control handle
while Shared still retains the owner. An admitted activation is allowed to
finish. Enforce the five-second input bound as a total monotonic deadline,
updating the remaining socket read timeout before every read; partial input
arriving repeatedly cannot reset the deadline or prolong shutdown. The signal
regression keeps sending partial input until the host closes the connection.
No budget or public door changes.

## 6. Devices and partitions

Every changed execution activation including A→B→A gets a fresh monotone semantic generation, never reuses a release number as a generation. Execution-content identity includes the prepared native module digest as well as schema/programs. A changed native digest advances the existing semantic generation atomically with release activation, even when native declarations are unchanged; removing a module changes the digest too. Equal execution content retains generation unless a release activation changes the final active/dormant population (§9.1), which requires a new replica generation; source-only activations still advance activation revision. The digest is SHA-256 over the exact prepared wasm bytes; the no-module sentinel is the empty string persisted as release_native_digest. activation_hash binds schema/programs and that digest. Comparison is with the current activation, so A→B→A is fresh. Startup attaches the persisted execution identity and verifies the immutable release module matches its recorded digest. This uses the existing device protocol, not a separate native epoch. Rollback and forward use the same generation replacement and full partition reacquisition already used by adoption: /schema advertises the exact active shape, sync rejects old watermarks, devices clear obsolete base/index state and rebase or refuse their queued intents under the existing authority/operation contract. Store id remains unchanged; this is not a store replacement. No dormant rows or fields enter snapshots, partition logs, online answers or device stores. Never combine old-generation pages with a new backend. A late old read remains tagged old and cannot overwrite a newly observed generation. Simulate rollback/forward across a delayed sync page and audience changes; retained data changes server storage only, not device persistence formats.

Lock order: command flock is CLI-only; serving control never acquires it. Candidate filesystem/native/worker preparation precedes engine lock. Serving publication holds engine → bound → generation → native, and separately replaces readers while engine is held (matching existing checkout); no /schema handler takes engine while holding bound. Ephemeral rebind uses its existing committed infallible path, never a fallible post-commit store read. Update desired worker root before revision; worker thread releases root/bound guards before taking engine. Wake/seq/answers follow existing publish_engine while engine is held: these are short memory updates/nonblocking condition notifications, required before new writer admission; no socket/child wait occurs there. `releases` includes the existing worker status metadata so post-commit launch failure is visible as degraded availability. No SQLite lock is held while waiting for a child process or socket peer.

## 7. Implementation and acceptance

Small commits: reviewed design; transactional retention/prune/refusals; local hot-control publication; integration/device/client coverage and docs. Authoring entry: SERVER.md owns the release-lifecycle section; guide evolution owns schema interactions. OPS guide deploy gets a one-line pointer to that section.

CLI matrix: `deploy <dir> [--restore-dormant] [--allow-feed-adoption]`; `rollback <dir> [--to N] [--allow-feed-adoption]`; `prune <dir> [--allow-destructive]`; `releases <dir>`. Legacy --allow-destructive on deploy/rollback is accepted but does not prune or bypass validation, and prints the prune command when relevant. --restore-dormant authorizes only restoration, never loss. --to requires a positive existing immutable release id and may select a newer release. E_ADOPT operator text: `table.column/index: N affected rows; sample ids [...]; values/keys [...]; reason; rewrite: <bridge/repair schema or command>; inspect: snapback4 sql <query> --release <dir>`. No flag bypasses invalid values, ownership or references. Unreachable live control refuses `E_STORE_BUSY: stop snapback4 serve <dir>, rerun the exact command, then snapback4 serve <dir>` before send; after send use `releases <dir>` and explicit `rollback --to N` after inspecting the outcome, never an automatic deploy allocating N+1.

History/status is page-based with `releases <dir> [--after <event-id>]`; bounded pages return a continuation and inventory pages use stable item keys. Prune audit stores a complete item list in SQLite; bounded replies carry a continuation rather than truncate silently. Critical assertions include T(a,b) → absent T → T(a) → absent T → T(a,b), preserved b and latest updated a; identical parent id/value replacement invalidates dormant edges; stale revision after A→B→A and equal-hash activation refuses; activation-time parent expiry followed by identical-id replacement invalidates dormant references; rapid B→C activation with a barrier between B worker preparation and credential minting discards B conventional/named source; an old checked-out reader not yet pinned retries the new factory including native-only changes; a connected client holding native module A hot-adopts B with unchanged declarations, reacquires B backend/module and refuses a delayed A result; inject interruption before/after commit and during pointer-copy updates to distinguish no activation from committed activation with an ambiguous result.

Tests also cover private socket permissions/symlinks/peer credentials, racing commands and writes during validation, process crash before commit and between commit/publication/reply, live prune/status, legacy empty dormancy and unchanged device format. Tests: deploy v1/write, v2 adding table/required default/enum/index/write, precise refusal while new enum is in use, authorized repair then rollback with dormant table/column, old update/insert/delete, forward restores prior values and supplies defaults only to new rows. Also prune preview/confirmation and asset lifetime; unique failure after dormant-index writes; required default-less forward refusal; transaction failure leaves active and dormant data intact; restart binds the committed release; simultaneous hot requests and failed hot activation; stale-generation sync and real client reacquisition; simulator delayed pages/audiences. Quality bar: the commissioned core/lang/device/sim cargo suites, CLI release/serve/adoption tests, relevant client tests and ref-check. No data-loss claim rests on diagnostics alone.

The coordinator subsequently authorized targeted confirmation, further review rounds and judgment calls, and commissioned implementation through tests and commits without another approval stop.

Targeted confirmation after continuation approval: both fresh Codex and Muse returned READY. Their verbatim responses are appended to the same provenance artifacts. Implementation follows this revision.

## 9. Implementation record

The core implements dormant tables/cells/index definitions and reference-lifetime evidence, partial restoration, required-value/enum/unique/cap refusal populations, prune/history, release-only format fencing, revision CAS and native digest in activation_hash. Nineteen focused retention regressions pass, including activation-time expiry, assets, typed audit pagination and ownership refusal.

The host implements the private same-owner transport, prepared native/worker custody, atomic live engine publication, reader fencing, revision-bound worker preparation, named-worker mutation and database-authoritative crash recovery. Seven focused hot-activation tests and four transport tests pass; the complete host unit suite passes (393 tests plus four existing ignored). All 23 release CLI/client integration tests pass, including an already-connected native-only client, concurrent traffic, hot narrowing refusal and process death before/after commit. Restoring full tables installs the identity scan before read-through adoption. Historical recorded attribution (including nulls), parent bindings and captured deadlines survive restoration; only physically missing captures are filled, and effective deadlines recompute under the candidate. An older release may make a through reference mutable; restoration refuses if its current parent table/id differs from the retained binding. Both naming orders of joint parent/child retirement, an unswept expired attribution target, effective deadline visibility, partial capture deletion/reuse and older retargeting are regressions. A test-only publication barrier permits an owned subprocess kill after commit and before runtime/pointer/reply publication, followed by database-authoritative recovery and retained forward restoration. Named secret selections are validated in immutable preparation before commit. The device and simulator exercise delayed old snapshots and audiences on both stores. SERVER.md owns the lifecycle semantics, the evolution card supplies the schema bridge, and the OPS deploy card is a one-line pointer. Final full quality results and commits are recorded in WORKSTREAM-REPORT.md. No publication is claimed.


Final complete core/lang/device/simulator command finished with exit 0. The 159-case consolidated client scope finished with 158 passing, one existing optional 0.2.32 interoperability skip and zero failures against an immutable built CLI. Final CLI, web/package and ref-check results are recorded in WORKSTREAM-REPORT.md. Internal name reservation is limited to `_dormant_` and `_release_`: established application underscore naming remains supported, while catalog-number resolution into r/i isolates physical internal tables. Both independent amendment reviewers returned READY. The implemented RFC is archived by removing its current/ link; this file remains at its permanent path.

## 8. Integration with the loop (2026-10-12)

Referential actions are selected with the active schema (LLP 3000.039). A
cascade/restrict declared only on a retired table/column is inactive while that
storage is dormant. The existing active-delete hook invalidates dormant edges;
restoration refuses E_ADOPT after deletion/reuse instead of applying a retired
action. If the reference remains active, the selected release's action applies,
including its default restrict. Retention cannot bypass active reference validation.

Union audiences (LLP 3000.036.000.000) and merged pages (LLP 3000.036.001) use
the ordinary generation reset and acquisition path. Reordered old pages refuse
E_GENERATION; a membership removed while the group arm is inactive cannot return
on roll forward. Activation's extracted builder preserves the audience-epoch
update for changed group declarations and the calendar schedule validity check.

Browser journal custody (LLP 3000.013.000 §17) remains tied to store and principal,
not release generation. Captured ids survive forward/rollback; current programs
rejudge an unexecuted intent, and receipts replay an executed one at most once.
A login epoch changes only with its authority, while personas retain epoch 0.
Serve refuses personas; the persona browser regression exercises the same
schema-generation changes through dev adoption, in both source directions.
OPS's deploy card retains account/admin/migration/port recipes and points to
SERVER.md#release-lifecycle for hot activation, retention, explicit restore/prune
and recovery. These are integration clarifications of implemented behavior.

An external database symlink does not move the control socket: it belongs to
the serving deployment's immutable-source directory. Another deployment sharing
that database is excluded rather than allowed to select its own source through
the first owner's socket. Its E_STORE_BUSY retains the canonical owner address
(or address-pending CLI holder), with the stop/retry remedy. Token forwarding
continues to use the canonical database owner. Both serving aliases are tested.

deadline and exact socket permissions). Fresh Codex and Muse final reviews

Both platforms pass frozen dependency installation, CLI/wasm/package builds
and the wasm budget. The dedicated Mac verification worktree is

## 9. Wave 9 release lifecycle amendment

Implementer: REL, 2026-10-12. This supersedes §3's narrowing refusal and §5's exposed request-generation race for release serving. HAND owns device version negotiation; DX owns production-writer diagnostics and starter/password UI.

### 9.1 Per-row narrowed-value dormancy

Each actual release activation, including roll-forward and equal-schema/program-only/native-only selection, runs the row-visibility closure. A visibility change advances generation even if execution hashes are equal, so devices replace their population; identical-source deploy no-ops do not activate. The closure removes authoritative rows whose **present** values do not fit the candidate column type or text constraint (asset leaves include ledger-backed kind, byte bound and file MIME allow-list, recursively through optionals/lists) into `_dormant_narrow_rows(t,id,data,definition,reason)`. Store format advances to `snapback4-release-store-3`; this binary opens format 2 and upgrades only during committed activation. Absent new required fields remain E_ADOPT. Engine metadata and maintained cache fields are not a narrowing trigger. Store the full authoritative image and its source table definition (merge restored whole-table and column definitions before classifying restored values), with interpreted old literal defaults and nulls, before projection. Restore any retained image that fits the candidate, splitting omitted columns into existing dormant cells. Restoration is automatic even on deploy; this is an existing row lifetime, not reuse of a removed schema name. No fallback syntax ships: the declared policy is hidden rows, never value substitution.

Visibility is a fixed point: a row with a non-null reference (including nested/list references) to a dormant narrowed row is also dormant. This covers authorization parents and memberships. Active programs, rules, point reads, lists, counts, caps, maintained views, assets and application SQL see only active r/i. Tentative restore-and-rehide work does not count as a visibility change; compute population change from newly hidden rows and successfully restored rows in the final closed population. All dependent physical indexes and aggregates rebuild when rows enter or leave dormancy, including otherwise unchanged tables. Sync generation replacement clears base/index images and acquisition, then reacquires only that same active population. This supersedes the device literal-default image-retention optimization: an additive default can restore a dormant cell whose actual value is incompatible, so schema shape alone cannot certify an old image. Same-generation attachment and identical-source no-ops preserve images; outbox custody survives generation replacement. HAND owns the shipped-device compatibility floor; DX owns updates to client/evolution guide text. A reference lookup to a dormant id is absent. Preserve LLP 3000.039 §1/§4.4: references validate a nominal id, not target existence. A new write may carry a dormant id just as it may carry an unknown/hidden id; read-through or explicit require/insert rules decide target admission. Such a write remains active under the old release until the next adoption's reference-closure pass, but grants no parent-derived authority. Never probe dormancy selectively at the reference write boundary, which would be an existence oracle. Dormant rows contribute neither to uniqueness nor caps. Restoration uses the same set-null cap exemption as ordinary writes; null-exempt groups are never counted by adoption or successor assessment. Their ids remain reserved: inserts cannot replace them and return the same E_RULE as a hidden live identity conflict, without row contents or a special dormant code. Updates/deletes by old programs see an absent row; active updates retain unrelated dormant cells. Dormant uniqueness keys are not reserved: old writes may cause a constraint conflict on forward, which E_ADOPT reports with executable application SQL; resolve that conflict under the current release when those rows are active. If both conflict rows are dormant, deploy a bridge with compatible widened types and the conflicting index non-unique/cap increased using --restore-dormant, restore both, repair with authorized mutations, then reinstate the constraint. Neither row silently wins; activation refuses atomically. Diagnostics include this bridge and application SQL, with values only in privileged operator output (§3). Type incompatibility alone never blocks switching.

At one pinned activation time, retire affected expired predecessor read-through rows under their old deadlines before narrowed hiding; retirement uses the ordinary authoritative delete to invalidate dependent evidence and remove dormant cells. Then restore candidate-compatible images, hide the incompatible/reference-dependent population, rebuild all physical r/i and _cap_counts/maintained caches, and validate before publication. Counts, sums and private parent-column mirrors all use this order. Reference closure removes at least one active row per iteration, terminates on the finite store even with nominal cycles, and any non-null element of a nested/list reference can hide its row. Null has no edge; a missing ordinary nominal target alone does not trigger hiding, while an absent target schema or narrowed-dormant target does. Capture dormant reference lifetime evidence before hiding; hiding is not deletion. Actual parent deletion invalidates evidence. Transferring a retained whole row or cell into narrowed dormancy preserves its existing bound/unbound/deleted evidence; it must never recapture a replacement parent. On candidate restoration, evidence invalidated by an actual deletion keeps a row dormant only if its reference/capture column is candidate-visible. An initially dangling nominal keep reference captures invalid=2 (unbound), distinct from invalid=1 (deleted captured lifetime); it may restore without inventing a lifetime. Deletion invalidates only bound (invalid=0) edges. Omitted reference cells and evidence stay retained for future restoration. A narrowed row never rebinds a deleted visible parent. Restored read-through images retain their historical captures and must pass the existing audience/deadline safety check. Whole-table restoration keeps the full source image through lifetime retirement and narrowed classification; ordinary candidate projection follows that boundary. Before projecting a narrowed image, evaluate retirement against its retained source definition/deadline, including direct expiry without read-through; removing read-through or expiry cannot revive an expired source lifetime. Actual retirement deletes the narrowed image, dependent captured evidence and its retained asset homes. Rows whose target table is absent stay dormant. Dormant rows run no jobs/expiry until reactivation. Assets remain retained but inaccessible. `prune --allow-destructive` drops narrowed images, associated dormant cells and lifetime evidence and releases only their asset homes; the preview names counts/bytes/sample ids. Inventory uses `rows:<table>` typed keys and audit items record before_rows, after_rows, restored_rows and bounded before_sample_ids in the activation transaction, including partial column/row restorations whose typed key remains. Per-table narrowed restoration counters distinguish restore-and-hide swaps with equal totals; summary restored_items includes partial restorations. Restoration identity sets are held transactionally and counted against final surviving r only after all read-through/cache activation phases. Column restored counts are recorded from cells actually consumed into surviving candidate rows, not inferred from inventory decreases; expiry/deletion may remove cells without restoring them. There is no automatic collection.

### 9.2 Exact preview and operator vocabulary

`rollback <dir> --to N --check` and `check --store <path> --release N` execute candidate adoption on a private SQLite snapshot created by VACUUM INTO through a read-only source connection. This is disk-backed, uses the exact adoption builder/validation, and never changes the live source, pointer, events or generation. SQLite supports VACUUM INTO on a read-only source; it acquires a coherent WAL read snapshot without the source writer lock. BUSY/disk failures refuse with retry advice, never fall back to copying sidecars. The temporary directory is owner-only and removed on success, refusal and ordinary unwinding. Process kill/crash may leave a private preview directory; operators can remove snapback4-release-preview-* leftovers when no preview uses them. The live format, including format 2, is never upgraded by preview. The snapshot may require disk/time proportional to the store; it provides a point-in-time plan, not a write freeze. The output enumerates the full candidate inventory and totals every narrowed population, not just the first sixteen status items; it includes any E_ADOPT refusal. Preview prepares the candidate native content identity but never starts workers or effects.

Deploy warns when type widening (including image/video byte bounds and file MIME/byte sets) admits values the previous release refuses; rollback also warns when its selected target admits values the current release refuses. All E_ADOPT inspect SQL uses quoted application table identifiers accepted by `sql`, including Quarry names that are SQL keywords; internal dormant inventory is inspected through `releases`. `latest` denotes the highest complete immutable source release, independently of current release and monotone generation. Complete here means a numeric directory with nonempty source that compiles; empty permanently reserved crash directories are skipped. Native preparation still validates a selected release before activation. Packaging and comparing the content digest precedes allocating a numeric release. Identical packaged source is a no-op: no new release directory, activation revision, audit or generation. A no-op is not an activation and therefore does not advance §5's activation revision. Widening warnings go to stderr, are advisory, and do not change a successful exit status. `releases <dir>` adds a JSON latest field; `releases <dir> [N|latest]` adds selected source id/digest/currentness without changing the authoritative current identity or activating; `rollback --to latest` and preview `--release latest` accept it. Command completion follows in-memory publication and prints `release N is current as generation G`.

### 9.3 HTTP admission and reporting

Integration 6: the generation footer shares the 16 MiB response budget; footer overflow replaces the partial payload with the ordinary bounded E_BOUND envelope. Unauthenticated 401 responses disclose no generation. The generation header is exposed through CORS on JSON, native and asset responses.

Buffer bounded request bytes before final release admission. A fair release request lease then spans each one-shot /q and /m's release-dependent lookup, typed argument decoding, execution and reply-identity capture. Release it before serialization/socket output, including every refusal/replay path. A scoped cleanup releases it on unwinding/early exits. Queued exclusive activation prevents new shared admissions, avoiding writer starvation. Release activation takes the exclusive lease across adoption and publication. Acquire it before engine/bound/readers locks. Sync and held polls retain their existing generation negotiation and wake behavior, so a long poll cannot block release publication. The guard admits concurrent readers; activation may pause new operations while it waits for current ones. Dev retains predecessor snapshot reading during adoption. Each engine JSON reply carries `generation`; custom application route bodies remain byte-owned by the application and carry the header. query, schema and sync envelopes that already specify their pinned generation retain that value; replay and generic refusals use the admitted request generation. Authentication, asset preparation/admission (including early refusals), durable actions/effects, service minting and ephemeral work share an engine-work accessor that stamps under the actual producer lock after buffered intake. Authentication enablement is rechecked against that same engine schema at credential collection/mint, including after KDF; successful ancillary credential checks and commit notifications do not relabel a captured result. Bound-only/raw metadata captures its own bundle identity. Durable actions stamp the engine that admits or collects after buffered intake, without holding a release lease during worker waits. Raw native/asset/following/application-route responses use the header, without modifying owned bytes. Stamp it while holding the engine that authorizes/collects the result, after body intake; a request-arrival stamp alone cannot label a later engine generation. The HTTP X-Snapback-Generation header carries the same value. Stream/asset replies use the header. Unknown operations return 404 denied E_OP; typed mutation argument decoding and range failures return 200 failed E_INPUT (with the request id when supplied); query argument failures and malformed JSON retain their 400 denied input envelope. No one-shot release operation exposes adoption loading images or E_GENERATION.

Default adoption output is one measured receipt line; phase/cache details are exposed with --verbose/--json. The check adoption reporter emits N_ADOPTION_PAUSE only when estimated work exceeds one second (explicit verbose/json retains the small-store estimate). Large unestimated work is still described in verbose output; no estimate is an SLO. guide deploy owns rollback-after-widening, business compatibility and persona-to-password onboarding text, with pointers to DX-owned first-writer checks/starters.


### 9.4 Round-one review dispositions

Muse (muse-spark-1.3-contributor, high) and a fresh GPT-6.1-sol (high) found the initial amendment NOT READY. Their verbatim records are under llp/reviews. Adopted: finite reference closure and rebuild ordering; ordinary hidden-id refusal; concrete bridge repair when both unique conflicts are dormant; VACUUM read-snapshot/error/crash semantics; precise latest/no-op/warning vocabulary; input buffering and reply lease release before socket I/O. GPT's nominal-reference objection is resolved by preserving LLP 3000.039's no-existence-check law. Its lifetime objections are resolved by separating initially dangling evidence, ignoring candidate-omitted edges, and retiring predecessor expiry before hiding. Round-two reviewers receive the revised amendment and implementation independently.


Round two: Muse READY; GPT NOT READY on four implementation gaps. Fixed and tested: prune/inventory asset homes now match retained row identities, equal-schema activations rerun closure and force a generation plus cache rebuild on population change, typed mutation decoding uses the promised failed envelope, and previews enumerate all inventory items. The GPT example alleging enum string rejection in wire::from_json was not accurate (the function converts text, and the engine validates the enum), but the broader wrong-shape/extra-argument envelope finding was valid and was fixed; the stress test now asserts the old-release enum failure unconditionally. Added focused tests for partial restoration/prune, legacy format-2 deletion evidence, equal-schema nominal references, mutation decoding and large inventories. Round three reviews the clarified amendment and these deltas independently.


Round three: Muse READY; GPT NOT READY on two additional restoration cases. Fixed: restored whole-table/column images now participate in closure even when absent from the immediate predecessor, using their retained source definitions; a tentative restore immediately hidden again does not advance generation. Regressions cover restore into a narrower table/column followed by another retirement/forward cycle, and equal-content reselection of a hidden parent/child with no visibility change. Round four independently checks the final amended rules and implementation.


Round four: Muse READY; GPT NOT READY on three combined cases. Fixed and tested: retained lifetime evidence survives transfer into narrowed storage (whole tables and cells, including deleted/reused parents), partial restorations remain explicit in paged audit items, and following/route responses stamp the executing/collecting engine generation after intake. Maintained cache fields are excluded from narrowing classification, as §9.1 states. A deterministic following request/intake switch regression joins the HTTP load test. Round five independently checks these corrections and the final revision.


Round five: Muse READY; GPT NOT READY on an audit decrease caused by expiry being mislabeled as restoration. Fixed with explicit committed column-consumption counters and an expiry regression. An additional device check found that the former literal-default image-retention optimization could present a now-dormant restored value under its fallback default. Generation replacement now always clears base/acquisition before reacquiring, on native and wasm, with a retired-column/literal-default regression on both stores; same-generation attachments and durable intents remain intact. Round six reviews these final corrections independently.

Round six: Muse READY; GPT NOT READY on disappearing column inventory keys bypassing consumption counts. Disappearing row/column keys now use the same actual restoration counters; whole-table restoration counts only original ids surviving in the final active population; removed-without-restoration items do not inflate restored_items. The expiry regression covers both omitted and reintroduced columns.

Round seven: Muse READY; GPT found expired narrowed images losing source deadlines when read-through is removed, initially dangling keep references misclassified during original whole-table capture, and action replies using intake generation. Fixed source-image retirement before projection, unbound classification at the original capture boundary, and action admission/collection stamps; regression coverage added. Audit counts and adoption receipts now use final surviving restoration identities after every activation phase.

Round nine: Muse READY; GPT found premature whole-table projection losing source deadlines, the source-image predicate omitting direct-expiry removal, and missing query arguments using the runtime 200 status. Preserved whole source images through classification, retired restored source lifetimes before hiding/projection (direct and inherited), and normalized query input refusals to the specified 400 envelope. New regressions cover expired and live full-table transfers and missing/non-object query args.

Round ten: Muse READY; GPT found asset byte/kind/MIME incompatibilities bypassing dormancy and later refusing activation. Classification now shares the existing ledger-backed asset validator for scalar/optional/list leaves on hiding and restoration. Author also verified a legal table named values broke unquoted inspection SQL; every hint now quotes the table identifier, with a live SQL regression. MEDIA retains upload/outbox ownership; this is release adoption only.

Round eleven: Muse READY; GPT found auth/guest minting under a new engine with the intake generation label. Producer capture is now shared across authentication and remaining worker/metadata paths, with intake-switch regressions. Explicit releases N/latest inspection fulfills the literal selector request in addition to the latest listing field.

Round twelve: Muse READY; GPT found null-exempt cap groups counted in adoption/assessment and upload preparation refusals missing producer capture. Reused the existing table cap exemption at both grouping sites and the shared producer accessor at every upload admission call. Regressions cover exempt/non-exempt restoration and a real Expect: 100-continue upload switched during intake.

## 10. Wave 10 release lifecycle amendment

Commission: Charlie's REL2 workstream, 2026-10-13; implementer Codex in
sb4-w10-rel2. This amends §9.2's definition of a complete release and §2's
capture restoration. No new language, planner or device protocol is introduced.

A newly packaged numeric source directory carries `.pending-adoption`, written
and fsynced before publication. It is selectable only if database activation
history proves that its adoption committed. Successful deploy removes the marker;
a crash, refused adoption or lost control reply leaves it. `latest`, numeric
rollback, release inspection and check-release all use this rule. The database
proof recovers a committed activation even if pointer/ledger completion failed,
and recognizes its committed predecessor as proof of legacy activation.
For retained stores, committed history gates unmarked directories too, excluding
refused candidates from older binaries. Legacy stores without release history
keep their complete-source selection rule. The marker is excluded from the
source digest. All production writes of numeric
release source go through deploy; rollback and serve only select existing source.
A refused candidate remains a reserved identity and can never become a selectable
release merely because it compiles. A matching-source dormant-name refusal leads
with `rollback <dir> --to latest`; other source uses `--restore-dormant`. Human
retention evidence lists counts/samples rather than serialized inventory JSON.

Bare rollback selects the previous release of the last committed activation,
including rollback and roll-forward; prune and same-release selections are excluded. Thus v1 → v2 → v3 → v1
followed by bare rollback activates v3. An explicit --to overrides this. Legacy
stores without activation history require explicit --to. A no-op adds no event.

`deploy --check` runs the exact candidate activation on the private snapshot with
**deploy** restoration intent, while rollback/check-release previews select
restoration. No candidate is selectable and live state remains unchanged.
Preview validates static worker prerequisites without starting workers. Successful
constraint narration comes from the validated candidate after dormant projection,
not the predecessor population. Successful previews describe dormant rows/cells with counts and bounded ids,
report successful maintain rebuild source visits, and omit speculative adoption
failures and capture-fill promises. Compatibility warnings are advisory, only for
populated affected tables, and include their current row count (not a prediction
that every row is incompatible). Preview flags removed schedules and populated
removed application tables as business safeguards absent from the target. Quarry
has no hold declaration: every removed populated table gets this advisory, so a
hold's name cannot evade it. Existing live writes can change any preview result.

Retired engine capture columns retain their source table definition alongside
cells. Restoring the same read-through definition uses the preserved capture and
parent lifetime, even when the intervening release widened its read rule. The
predecessor audience correspondence barrier applies only to rows requiring new
captures. Historical rows must still pass retained reference lifetime and
capture/parent validation. A changed retained read-through definition refuses;
new rows without historical captures retain the existing first-adoption checks.
Source expiry is evaluated before projection, so restoration cannot revive an
expired captured lifetime. Recorded attribution keeps its historical value.

`run <job> --release <dir>` routes to the served store at its canonical loopback
address using `SNAPBACK4_SERVICE_TOKEN` or `--token-env NAME`; it needs no local
project/source. `POST /jobs/<job>` requires a full service capability, rechecked
under the enqueueing engine lock, and executes system work on the existing FIFO.
Every full service seat is privileged to trigger any system job; operators must
provision these tokens accordingly. Accounts, guests, read scopes and personas
are refused. `/dev/jobs` stays development-only. The reply waits for done/parked;
a lost reply is ambiguous and does not authorize automatic retry. It adds no job
priority, worker pool, token scope or idempotency contract.

`serve.lock` remains an address hint. Only successful sidecar ownership allows
reclamation. After acquiring it, an ESRCH result from kill(pid,0) identifies a dead
record and emits a diagnostic with PID, URL and directory before overwriting it.
A live/reused PID or uncertain probe never authorizes signalling or lock bypass.

Acceptance: v1/v2/rollback/refused-v3/latest and explicit refused selection;
non-numeric-history rollback; deploy preview state equality; empty/populated
warnings; all packaged rollback claims; old release E_OP/NOT_FOUND/null and exact
forward values; retained captures after widening plus deleted/reused parent and
new-row refusal; service-token job completion and unauthorized/read-only requests;
stale-lock recovery and live-owner conflict provenance. HS owns handshake/types;
DX2 owns starters/client recipes; DIAG and PAGE2 own language/planner diagnostics.


REL2 review round one: fresh GPT-6.1-sol found legacy predecessor selection,
pre-dormancy constraint narration and omitted static worker prerequisite checks.
All three were reproduced and corrected with migration, unique/narrowing and
worker-selection regressions. Review artifacts retain the verbatim findings.


REL2 round two: [Muse](./reviews/3000.000.000-rel2-wave10-r2.muse.md) and
[fresh Codex](./reviews/3000.000.000-rel2-wave10-r2.codex.md) both READY on
this amendment and implementation. Focused final verification passes. Informational
receipt sampling also covers populated items beyond empty inventory definitions;
guide consistency lives in its own test module, preserving the pre-existing
parent test file's line count. No publication or push was performed by REL2.
