 succeeded in 0ms:
# LLP 3000.011.005.000: Qualify the built application on its actual hosts

**Type:** RFC
**Status:** Accepted
**Systems:** Snapback 4 application witnesses, Expo Release builds, deployments, provider effects, benchmark records
**Author:** Codex / GPT-6 (for Charlie Cheever)
**Date:** 2026-09-22
**Revised:** 2026-09-29 (served-generation and kept-fact binding for generic native smoke; real-provider exercise remains conditional and unrun)
**Related:** [Problem](./3000.011.005-native-and-production-evidence.research.md), [LLP 3000.009](./3000.009-general-purpose-backend.rfc.md), [LLP 3000.008.003](./3000.008.003-whole-application-performance.rfc.md)

## 1. Recommendation

Use the existing published witness on the application the builder actually
produced, with its exact installed packages. Add the missing application
assertions and run them on an installed native Release build. Then perform
scoped deployment/restore and provider exercises using existing tools.
Record each boundary independently in the existing benchmark run directory.

This proposal adds execution and application integration rather than a second
harness, registry, certification badge or universal publication gate. A
successful native run does not automatically qualify deployment or providers.

## 2. Native application witness

Build and install the Expo app with the actual published native module on a
dedicated compatible simulator. Use the existing `snapback4-expo/witness`
helper and `snapback4 witness ios`; a Hermes export and the Expo Go online
fallback do not satisfy this step. Keep the real screen visible so receipts
and recorded UI refer to the same application behavior.

Prime authorized history, search and viewed ordinary media online. Terminate
the app, stop the backend and verify network failure. Cold-launch and assert
held history/search/media, incomplete horizons where applicable, and no
durable replay of consumed view-once media. Queue two messages and persist
their ids; terminate and relaunch offline, then verify those same pending
intents survived. Reconnect, observe ordered authoritative settlement and
receipt progression without duplicate rows. Learn a membership revocation,
relaunch offline and verify its retained-history/new-delivery boundary.

Use the driver's existing failure receipts, video, logs and run manifest.
The manifest must identify the installed app, native package, backend package,
simulator runtime, origin and exact artifacts. Missing artifacts remain
missing, and retries receive fresh directories. A simulator result is still
not a physical-device, Android or power-loss result.

### Generic smoke, 2026-09-24

`witness ios --smoke` adds a no-app-code first rung to the existing driver.
On an explicit smoke launch the Expo device attaches to the application's
own local client (including `createApp`/`AppProvider`), installs the native
JS error handler and reads counts and sync identity from the kept device.
Ordinary launches do not load the runner. It does not sign in for the app.
Prime waits for online acquisition; a cold offline launch checks persisted
counts and all optional-argument queries from the kept generated program
metadata; restart requires online sync through the exact driver barrier. No query rows enter the
receipts. Screenshots/video still show the real app's visible content.

After backend readiness, the driver reads the committed compiler generation
and binds it to the owned host's reported generation/store identity. It sends
that generation and the complete eligible query facts in the launch payload.
Before any query, the helper requires every runs/held fact to agree with its
kept backend and both device/backend generations to match the launch. The
driver refuses generation/fact changes across restart and checks the receipt
generation; a separate compilation of live source cannot supply these facts. Each smoke receipt records those facts and each
executed query's classification and actual result. Missing facts or a different
eligible operation set fail; classification is never inferred from `loading`.
Device-answerable queries (`runs: device`, including held reads) require data
or an explained refusal offline. Loading, a throw or an uncaught error fails.
Compiler-declared server-only reads (`runs: server`, including ephemeral and
online-only tables) are recorded offline as `online-only: unavailable offline
(expected)`, with their actual `loading` or `E_OFFLINE`-style refusal. This is
expected unavailability, not an answer. Those same eligible server-only queries
must answer in both online prime and restart. Required-argument queries remain
outside generic smoke. Optional-argument native queries are also refused by
generic smoke: their compiler server bit describes conditional module residency,
not unconditional server-only behavior. Use an application witness for those
queries. The native client must still acquire and reopen its
partition; an online-only client fallback cannot satisfy smoke.


A quiet backend's commit watermark cannot advance from reads alone. While
its owned backend and app are stopped, the driver records one zero-write
commit, with no application row, schema, log event or outbox change. The
manifest names this sync marker and all three segment receipts, timings and
screenshots. This is lifecycle evidence, not a substitute for the deeper
application assertions above; empty partitions and required-argument queries
remain explicitly limited evidence.

Round-one review hardening binds restart to the barrier sequence in both the
launch configuration and driver validation, so a pre-barrier tail cannot
stand in for reconnect progress. A successful segment's owned PID must still
exist at deliberate termination. Device state is reread after the render
pause before attesting counts and identity. `createApp` notifies the native
host only after publishing a client; the runner follows replacements and
cancels a closed client's in-flight sample without a failure receipt.

## 3. Acquisition on the same app

Run the full retained fixture on the actual client, identifying the offline
content required before measuring. Record first useful render, completed
durable acquisition, transferred rows/bytes, disk size and peak memory where
the host exposes it, then prove offline reopen. If peak memory cannot be
measured, name the absent metric; do not substitute JavaScript heap for all
native or wasm memory.

Preserve LLP 3000's physical-floor comparison only for equivalent fixture,
host and persistence semantics. Smaller reference-app timings and server
query latencies do not substitute for this measurement. These observations
also evaluate the residency proposal, without making it responsible for
native module correctness.

## 4. Deployment, restore and provider boundaries

Use an isolated disposable deployment with the existing release commands.
Exercise password session recovery, an asset read, schema adoption, a queued
job across process restart and the documented stopped backup/restore path.
Keep restored workers and old production clients fenced; do not reconnect a
prior outbox and claim recovery merely because fresh reads work. Test rollback
as source activation, not database rewind. Record the storage mode and the
precise crash class; this is not physical-power-loss qualification.

For one real push-provider adapter, select a test account/device and explicit
recipient before sending. The user-authorized implementation, publication
and benchmark program does not by itself identify a provider account or a
message recipient. Prepare the adapter and test-double crash cases first;
execute actual external delivery only with that account/recipient authorization.
No secret values belong in artifacts or LLPs.

Distinguish effect admission, provider acceptance and device receipt. Inject
the acceptance-before-local-settlement failure using a labeled test double;
then verify the real provider's supported deduplication/outcome behavior.
If a provider cannot deduplicate or resolve an uncertain result, record the
remaining ambiguity instead of claiming exactly-once external delivery.

## 5. Benchmark integration and availability

Before launching the next comparable Clique run, record package pins, builder
and grader identities, required simulator availability and the committed
probe set, or explicitly declare any protocol deviation. Preserve the frozen
instrument and perform native qualification as an explicit extension after
the cold build and independent grading. The prior builder prompt expressly
made simulator proof optional. Do not change that timed task silently. Do not quietly count a new
native proof as though previous runs used the same environment.

Append a compact coverage table to the existing report: scenario, host,
artifact link, result and limitation. A missing credential, simulator or
signing identity is an execution prerequisite, not a passing product test.
Continue independent browser/server work while that cell remains unavailable;
do not manufacture its outcome or replace it with a compilation check.

## 6. Completion and limits

The native increment is complete when the actual application passes its
installed Release witness and every required artifact is retained. Deployment
and provider increments complete separately on their stated environments.
Any discovered defect receives a reproduction, an affected fix and a rerun
of that boundary before the claim changes.

Run slow host exercises outside the ordinary edit/push loop and on a machine
whose simulator use will not disrupt the user. Use the existing fleet and
owned-process rules. No full battery at an ever-moving main is required.
Do not spend time building more evidence infrastructure when an existing
witness can execute the missing scenario directly.

## 7. Implementation and evidence checkpoint

The bounded operational increment is implemented in the existing
`packages/snapback4/test/release-activation.test.mjs` and `worker.test.mjs`,
without a new harness or provider framework. On the isolated M5 checkout,
the two affected deployment tests and two provider-double tests passed.
Password login/rotation survives the stopped full-deployment copy; the copied
asset, fresh process device, schema adoption and source-only rollback pass.
A genuinely queued scheduled job survives an owned serving process's SIGKILL
before its first chunk and resumes under the same ID exactly once.

The loopback provider double accepts before an owned worker is killed, before
either step or effect settlement. The successor's stable-key reconciliation
produces two submissions and one accepted delivery when the double supports
it. When the outcome is unavailable, it parks without a second submission.
Both paths refuse stale claims. These are process-crash/stopped-copy tests on
SQLite WAL with `synchronous=NORMAL`, not power-loss, real-provider or device
delivery evidence. Old production clients/outboxes and restored effect
workers remain disconnected by the exercise's explicit isolation procedure.

The [execution record and native adaptation plan](../snapback4/reports/20260922-application-evidence.md)
retain commands, timings, artifact locations and coverage limits. A reference
Release witness passed both offline and kill/relaunch segments against the
rebuilt workspace artifact before and after the sign-out review fix; it remains distinct
from the required actual application and an installed npm package. Cal16's frozen
instrument and cold builder packet were not modified. Its cold build and
independent grading are now complete: 104 assertions passed, zero failed and
15 remain unrun; the independent holistic score is 88. The separate extension
uses a preserved copy of the completed app and final M8 fixture, the existing
helper, real app screens and exact published pins. Actual-app installation,
full retained acquisition and the real-provider cell were still pending at
that checkpoint. The candidate native result below advances that evidence;
final public pins and the real-provider cell remain distinct prerequisites.

## 8. Actual-app qualification exposed a native media defect

The [post-grade native failure](../snapback4/reports/20260922-native-media-repair.md)
reached the installed Expo 57 Release app using the published 0.2.32 client
and 0.2.23 native package. After ordinary native acquisition, a successful
HTTP 200 image response produced a 2,062-byte React Native Blob without
`arrayBuffer()`. The ordinary cache calls that method unconditionally, so
the actual image/video hooks refused and no ordinary bytes reached the cache.
The failed `restart-3` attempt remains evidence against the original package;
earlier signing/entitlement setup failures are recorded separately.

The selected remedy stays in the shared asset implementation: use the host's
public `FileReader.readAsArrayBuffer` only when Blob lacks its direct method,
and explicitly own/release response Blobs through the host's available APIs.
Keep once responses outside durable byte extraction, retain caller ownership
of upload Blobs, and preserve browser URL behavior. Native handle release
does not promise instantaneous memory zeroization or removal of decoder copies.
No app shim, native ABI change, new dependency or resource ceiling is needed.
Astra xhigh implements the repair; both requested reviewer families and exact
installed-package native reruns must precede a success claim. The original
cal16 app, grade and score remain frozen across the corrective release.


The first candidate passed ordinary native rendering, then exposed a second
host incompatibility: Expo 57's object-URL polyfill threw while its expected
bridgeless native proxy was absent. A separate ordinary-Blob probe reproduced
the exception with the link online and without consuming another once grant.
The selected transient fallback is public `FileReader.readAsDataURL` when
object-URL creation fails. It copies response bytes into an in-memory base64
URI and never writes them to media storage or refetches the asset. Browser
object URLs remain preferred. Copied data strings cannot be revoked, so the
caller must drop them on screen close; SDK cleanup releases its owned
references and native Blob, not arbitrary copies. This documented tradeoff
preserves the server's single grant and the no-durable-once-storage contract;
it adds neither a private native dependency nor a native ABI. Review and
qualification must check once-file/manifest absence while the actual image is
open as well as after close and process death. The [repair record](../snapback4/reports/20260922-native-media-repair.md)
separates both failures, patches and pending qualification.


Once rendering then passed, including live-handle durable-storage exclusion,
but the actual video player exposed a separate native-cache contract defect.
The valid original MP4 and cached file were byte-identical. AVFoundation decoded
that content with a `.mp4` filename and refused its extensionless cache name.
The selected first-party Swift storage repair gives ordinary MP4 a real suffix,
resolves/migrates legacy extensionless MP4, and keeps atomic writes, reads and
purges coherent across owned variants. Existing device locking, ID validation,
image storage and once exclusion remain; no app display-copy workaround or
Rust/C ABI change is introduced. Expo advances to 0.2.24 and the consumer's
Swift is rebuilt, while the Rust frameworks remain unchanged. Actual native
video/restart/revocation and final published-pin evidence are still required.


The combined 0.2.33 SDK / 0.2.24 Expo candidate then passed the actual Clique
Release application's restart, revocation and full-acquisition scenarios.
Restart includes fresh native offline image/video rendering on both process
launches, the exact two pending intents surviving death, exactly two ordered
sent settlements, and delivered/read progression. Revocation retains authorized
text history but purges ordinary media and excludes later delivery offline
and after an explicit reconnect. Full acquisition enumerates all 10,000
required message IDs in 100 scopes across 299 actual local query pages while
offline, before reconnect, matching the frozen fixture's canonical ID hash.
The [native application report](../snapback4/reports/20260922-clique-native-extension.md)
retains exact timings, package/app custody and limitations. These are candidate
results with the original 0.2.32 CLI. At that candidate checkpoint the final
published 0.2.33/0.2.24 pair still required installed-package requalification;
§9 records its subsequent completion. These observations do not change cal16's
original grade or qualify a physical device, Android, power loss or real provider.

## 9. Public-package completion and remaining provider cell

The final [actual Clique public-package qualification](../snapback4/reports/20260922-clique-native-extension.md#final-public-package-result)
passed all three unchanged native scenarios after registry installation of
SDK/CLI 0.2.33 and Expo 0.2.24, with all qualified integrities matched. The
normal Release build and its native inputs are recorded independently of the
earlier candidates. Exact offline image/video rendering, live once exclusion,
pending/settled/delivered/read pairs, learned revocation, and the 10,000-ID
offline acquisition proof all passed. The source repair, both review families,
failed attempts and final publication remain linked in the repair report.

| Increment | Actual result and boundary |
| --- | --- |
| Actual application on public native packages | Complete: three installed iOS simulator Release scenarios pass on SDK/CLI 0.2.33 and Expo 0.2.24. Physical-device, Android and power-loss evidence is absent. |
| Deployment, stopped restore and queued-job restart | Complete in the [operational record](../snapback4/reports/20260922-application-evidence.md), including isolated restore and process crash/restart. This does not establish power-loss durability. |
| Provider test doubles | Complete for recorded acceptance-before-worker-crash, stable-key deduplication and uncertain-outcome boundaries. A test-double acceptance is not a real provider or device receipt. |
| Real provider acceptance and device delivery | **Unrun**, conditional on the selected adapter, test account/device and recipient required by §4. |

This RFC stays `Accepted` for the independent real-provider increment. The
completed native and operational increments do not depend on inventing that
missing evidence. Cal16's original grade/score and all unrun labels remain
unchanged. The separate [final cold corrective-release benchmark](../benchmarks/runs/2026-09-22-snapback4-clique-astra-0.2.33/README.md)

# Rules

**Snapback 2 optimizes the edit-to-verified loop — the agent's building an app on it, and ours building it.**

Speed is the constraint. Everything below exists to keep the edit-to-verified loop short
and to stop this repository accreting apparatus faster than anyone can remove it. These
rules bind `snapback2/` and the LLPs numbered 1000 and up; Snapback 1 is baselined, not
exempt, and its numbers may only fall.

Each rule is **[check]** (enforced by `caps`) or **[review]** (enforced by a human).

## Budgets — every budget is a trade, not a limit

- **5 blocking checks, 60s total.** A sixth requires deleting one, in the same PR. **[check]**
- **59 documents in the working set** (`llp/current/`). Today's count. A 60th requires
  archiving one — which is just removing the link. Snapback 2 aims to leave this at 15;
  Snapback 1's links are archived at promotion. **[check]**
- **12 documents in the foundation** (`llp/foundation/`). **[check]**
- **This file: 700 words.** If it grows, something becomes a check or stops being a rule. **[check]**
- **1,500 lines per source file, baseline 204.** Generated files exempt — and generated
  files are built, not committed; so are benchmark run records under `benchmarks/runs/`,
  which are evidence, not source. The baseline is Snapback 1's oversize count on the day
  the rule landed; it may not grow, and `snapback2/` contributes zero to it. **[check]**

## Blocking checks

`caps` · `ref-check` · `snapback2/scripts/test.sh` · `snapback2/scripts/measure.mjs --check` · `check-inventories`

## Loop shape

- **Every check reports all failures in one run.** Fail-fast is banned. **[check]**
- **Nothing blocks on anything slower than 60 seconds.** Slow verification runs on a
  schedule, attributed to the breaking commit, and files a bounded set of issues. **[review]**
- **Fix loops get 3 rounds.** Then stop: escalate to a human or descope. **[review]**

## Time budgets

Tracked every run of `snapback2/scripts/measure.mjs` (appends `snapback2/metrics/loop.jsonl`).
A regression is a P0 with a name on it.

| | budget |
|---|---|
| Incremental rebuild after a one-line edit | 15s |
| Test what you just changed | 30s |
| Blocking gate suite | 60s |
| Full build, warm cache | 5 min |
| **`snapback2 check` on a program (the agent's product latency)** | **1s** |
| **`snapback2 doctor` boot-to-ready** | **0.5s** |

## Scope

- **`NOT-DOING.md` is binding.** Moving something onto the doing-list means writing why
  and taking something off. **[review]**
- **Delete; don't deprecate.** No compat shims or migration paths before promotion. **[review]**
- **Nothing is compiled, transpiled, or generated at runtime that could be built ahead of
  time.** **[review]**
- **A spec needs an implementer and a date, or it isn't written.** **[review]**
- **Only implemented specs bind.** A document with no running code behind it is a
  proposal, and a proposal cannot block a PR or justify a check. **[review]**

## Agents

- **Agents remove apparatus freely and add none.** An agent PR that adds a check, script,
  registry, config file, or design doc needs explicit human approval saying so. **[review]**
- **2026-09-06 session exception:** the user delegated the search implementation
  decisions; one `snapback2-core/build.rs` may generate pinned Unicode tables at
  build time, alongside the existing package build. No new gate, registry,
  runtime generator, or Unicode dependency is authorized by this exception. **[review]**
- **Snapback 2 LLPs are numbered 1000 and up**; `llp-create` must be told. **[review]**
- **Snapback 4 LLPs are numbered 3000 and up** (`snapback4/`, LLP 3000); it adopts these budgets
## Summary

Snapback 4 is one of two parallel candidates for the successor to Snapback 2,
and the sibling of Snapback 3, not its successor. It builds
[LLP 2000.000](./2000.000-keep-the-core-flip-two-substrates.rfc.md) as
written: the Snapback 2 core kept verbatim, two substrates flipped up front —
resources metered at runtime instead of priced statically, and a client that
replicates the viewer's declared partition instead of caching receipted
results — proved first on the hub fixture, with a deterministic simulator as
the primary harness. Snapback 3 ([LLP 2000](./2000-snapback3.rfc.md), led by
Codex / GPT-6 Astra) keeps the same two questions open and discovers their
answers in its first slice. Snapback 2 ([LLP 1000](./1000-snapback2.explainer.md))
continues as the incumbent and the control. All three are judged by one
pre-registered comparison on one clock.

This document records the author's decisions of 2026-09-12 that created the
track, restates the bet on one page so a reader can orient without the brief,
fixes the bakeoff protocol, draws the fence, and plans the first four weeks.
The design argument and its evidence stay in LLP 2000.000. Only running code
will bind (`rules/RULES.md`).

## 1. Decisions (author, 2026-09-12)

On 2026-09-12 Charlie Cheever asked whether Claude Fable 5.1 should lead a
Snapback 4 while Codex / GPT-6 Astra leads Snapback 3 and Snapback 2
continues, "and see which one turns out best." The lead answered yes with
conditions; the author accepted them ("Sounds good") and asked for this
document. The decisions below are therefore the lead's proposal as accepted
by the author. Where the author has not separately ruled, the text says so.

1. **Three tracks, one comparison.** Snapback 2 continues as the incumbent
   and the control. Snapback 3 builds LLP 2000; Snapback 4 builds LLP
   2000.000. This is a bakeoff of *bets* — discover the substrates in the
   slice versus decide them first — not of models. The result will say more
   about the bets than about the leads, and §3 is written so that it can.
2. **The name is Snapback 4.** Siblings, not a sequence: "4" does not mean
   "after 3." A separate number gives the track its own corpus root,
   directory, package identity and data directory with no rename when the
   bakeoff resolves, and files never move in LLP. If Snapback 4 loses, it is
   a number that never shipped. "Snapback 3b" was considered and rejected:
   it reads as a variant of 3, and this is a different bet.
3. **Snapback 4 builds LLP 2000.000 as written.** Its §14 open questions are
   the lead's to decide inside the track, recorded in §7 as they are
   decided. The author rules on the bar, the comparison and promotion (§3).
4. **One owner on the core.** The lead writes the engine, the store and the
   client contract. Codex lanes build the fixture generator, the reference
   applications, the device witnesses and the periphery. Blind reviews from
   two model families are reserved for the two disclosure-sensitive
   mechanisms: partition inversion of read rules (LLP 2000.000 §7.1) and
   the meter's cut behaviour on non-uniform scans (LLP 2000.000 §6). This
   departs from the standing rule that Codex codes and Claude orchestrates,
   deliberately, for the core only.
5. **The clock is four weeks** from the first commit under `snapback4/` to
   the first slice running on the hub fixture. At the clock the author kills
   the track, merges its substrates into a survivor, or continues it. There
   is no third month without a ruling.
6. **Shared and not shared.** Shared with Snapback 3: the LLP corpus, the
   Clique harness and scorers, the fixtures, the briefs, and the simulator
   once either track has one. Not shared: code, except the Snapback 2 pieces
   both are expected to copy (LLP 2000.000 §5.1).
7. **Snapback 2 keeps shipping and freezes new substrate work for the
   duration.** Fix-forward, the loop bar, acquisition and papercuts continue;
   no new certificate axes, client custody mechanisms or ceiling changes.
   This is accepted here as a condition of the bakeoff. Making it binding on
   concurrent Snapback 2 sessions needs one line in `rules/NOT-DOING.md`'s
   Moves ledger, which this document does not write.
8. **Reading load.** This root, updated in place, and one append-only
   journal, `snapback4/JOURNAL.md`. Sub-LLPs only for a decision that must
   outlive the journal. No panels beyond decision 4's two reviews.

## 2. The bet on one page
## 4. Fence, numbering, routing

- **Directory.** `snapback4/`, with its own Cargo workspace and Bun
  workspace, excluded from the root workspace as `snapback2/` is. No
  Snapback 1 or Snapback 2 gate, inventory, backstop, registry or script
  applies; the pre-push hook selects none.
- **Identities.** Binary and npm shim `snapback4`; data directory
  `.snapback4/`; ephemeral ports; its own personas, credentials and process
  ownership. It never depends at runtime on Snapback 2 internals. It copies
  source deliberately, split under the 1,500-line cap on the way in.
- **Numbering.** LLPs 3000 and up; sub-LLPs `3000.NNN`; `llp-create
  --under 3000`. The 2000 series remains Snapback 3's and the 1000 series
  Snapback 2's. Design work for Snapback 4 extends this root or LLP 2000.000
  before it creates a new number.
- **Rules.** `rules/RULES.md` binds "LLPs numbered 1000 and up" by its
  letter, as LLP 2000 §9 noted for its own series. Snapback 4 adopts its
  budgets and loop shape as its own: five blocking checks in sixty seconds,
  `check` under a second, `doctor` under half a second, 1,500 lines per
  file, a bounded working set, delete rather than deprecate, only
  implemented specs bind. Its not-doing list is `snapback4/NOT-DOING.md`,
  written with the directory. The root `AGENTS.md`/`CLAUDE.md` routing line
  and the rules fence note land with the directory, not with this document.
- **Publication.** From week one, `0.0.x`, one script; the benchmark runs
  the published pin.

## 5. The first four weeks
9's final Linux artifact is +5,258 B raw / +1,127 B gzip, without per-feature
attribution or changed runtime ceilings. Retain 0.4.27: PAGE3 ships with wave-11
in 0.4.28. Mac suite is deferred to that publication; no push. Linux full
qualification and regenerated census evidence follow in a separate commit.

Integration 10 qualification repairs: the native 20k campaign found nine
opaque-cursor byte comparisons between PAGE3 direct and let-hoisted plans.
The let oracle now follows each plan's own cursor, comparing every page and
cursor presence. Its bound uses the post-prelude physical store population;
three actual regressions cover the original seed, prelude-created rows and
missing cursor/data/completeness/refusal/bad-resumption mutants. Codex round 1
found the initial seeded-row bound; round 2 and Muse approve the corrected
implementation and six-execution regression. Both verbatim rounds are retained
under llp/reviews/3000.035-integration10-cursors.*. The final native campaign
has 20k compiled cases, 1,135,558 executions and zero disagreements.

The old sequence-disclosure scan expected eight probes. PAGE3's two owner
ranges each reserve its two static probes once, totaling four independently
of hidden witness lifetimes. Retain every paired-world outcome comparison,
next-expiry assertion and the eight-probe write reservation. The repaired
existing regression passes. No engine or runtime ceiling changed.

Integration 10 also regenerates word-expansion.json via
WORD_EXPANSION_UPDATE_FIXTURE: PAGE3's pure-membership planner now merges the
blank-search browse arm, with its existing capability facts and inferred unique
source constraint. All thirteen fixture populations retain identical authorized
pages and seeded row bytes; only compiled backends change. The native generator
and no-update comparison pass. No fixture was hand-merged.

Integration 10's final full simulator run exposed a compiler interaction:
PAGE3 expanded the pure-membership empty-search browse arm, breaking the
existing paired-scan recognition and disabling nonempty search inference.
Keep compiler-generated pure-membership search variants out of branch page
expansion, using a separate site set so equality/order pin handling stays
unchanged. Plain membership lists and owner/compound PAGE3 families remain
eligible. The unchanged 99,090-row case, all four read-cut controls and ten
word-expansion device tests pass. Regenerate word-expansion.json again: this
supersedes the earlier temporary backend changes and restores its original
main bytes. The compiler regression checks ordinary inference beside a PAGE3
list merge. Fresh Codex and Muse both mark this final patch READY.

The wave-1 missing-pin diagnostic now uses an arithmetic filter to retain its
negative coverage; its original pure-membership page is asserted note-free.
Final rebuilt native/device/Lean campaigns retain the same totals and no new
signatures, and the farm remains 2 notes in one original schema / 0 repaired.
Every orchestrated Cargo qualification carries the three requested profile
variables. Independent review logs retain their actual commands; Codex's
additional review-side test invocations used the default profile rather than
the explicitly requested environment, a delegation-brief omission. Future
review briefs must pass those Cargo constraints explicitly.

# LLP 3000.011.005.000: Qualify the built application on its actual hosts

**Type:** RFC
**Status:** Accepted
**Systems:** Snapback 4 application witnesses, Expo Release builds, deployments, provider effects, benchmark records
**Author:** Codex / GPT-6 (for Charlie Cheever)
**Date:** 2026-09-22
**Revised:** 2026-09-29 (served-generation and kept-fact binding for generic native smoke; real-provider exercise remains conditional and unrun)
**Related:** [Problem](./3000.011.005-native-and-production-evidence.research.md), [LLP 3000.009](./3000.009-general-purpose-backend.rfc.md), [LLP 3000.008.003](./3000.008.003-whole-application-performance.rfc.md)

## 1. Recommendation

Use the existing published witness on the application the builder actually
produced, with its exact installed packages. Add the missing application
assertions and run them on an installed native Release build. Then perform
scoped deployment/restore and provider exercises using existing tools.
Record each boundary independently in the existing benchmark run directory.

This proposal adds execution and application integration rather than a second
harness, registry, certification badge or universal publication gate. A
successful native run does not automatically qualify deployment or providers.

## 2. Native application witness

Build and install the Expo app with the actual published native module on a
dedicated compatible simulator. Use the existing `snapback4-expo/witness`
helper and `snapback4 witness ios`; a Hermes export and the Expo Go online
fallback do not satisfy this step. Keep the real screen visible so receipts
and recorded UI refer to the same application behavior.

Prime authorized history, search and viewed ordinary media online. Terminate
the app, stop the backend and verify network failure. Cold-launch and assert
held history/search/media, incomplete horizons where applicable, and no
durable replay of consumed view-once media. Queue two messages and persist
their ids; terminate and relaunch offline, then verify those same pending
intents survived. Reconnect, observe ordered authoritative settlement and
receipt progression without duplicate rows. Learn a membership revocation,
relaunch offline and verify its retained-history/new-delivery boundary.

Use the driver's existing failure receipts, video, logs and run manifest.
The manifest must identify the installed app, native package, backend package,
simulator runtime, origin and exact artifacts. Missing artifacts remain
missing, and retries receive fresh directories. A simulator result is still
not a physical-device, Android or power-loss result.

### Generic smoke, 2026-09-24

`witness ios --smoke` adds a no-app-code first rung to the existing driver.
On an explicit smoke launch the Expo device attaches to the application's
own local client (including `createApp`/`AppProvider`), installs the native
JS error handler and reads counts and sync identity from the kept device.
Ordinary launches do not load the runner. It does not sign in for the app.
Prime waits for online acquisition; a cold offline launch checks persisted
counts and all optional-argument queries from the kept generated program
metadata; restart requires online sync through the exact driver barrier. No query rows enter the
receipts. Screenshots/video still show the real app's visible content.

After backend readiness, the driver reads the committed compiler generation
and binds it to the owned host's reported generation/store identity. It sends
that generation and the complete eligible query facts in the launch payload.
Before any query, the helper requires every runs/held fact to agree with its
kept backend and both device/backend generations to match the launch. The
driver refuses generation/fact changes across restart and checks the receipt
generation; a separate compilation of live source cannot supply these facts. Each smoke receipt records those facts and each
executed query's classification and actual result. Missing facts or a different
eligible operation set fail; classification is never inferred from `loading`.
Device-answerable queries (`runs: device`, including held reads) require data
or an explained refusal offline. Loading, a throw or an uncaught error fails.
Compiler-declared server-only reads (`runs: server`, including ephemeral and
online-only tables) are recorded offline as `online-only: unavailable offline
(expected)`, with their actual `loading` or `E_OFFLINE`-style refusal. This is
expected unavailability, not an answer. Those same eligible server-only queries
must answer in both online prime and restart. Required-argument queries remain
outside generic smoke. Optional-argument native queries are also refused by
generic smoke: their compiler server bit describes conditional module residency,
not unconditional server-only behavior. Use an application witness for those
queries. The native client must still acquire and reopen its
partition; an online-only client fallback cannot satisfy smoke.


A quiet backend's commit watermark cannot advance from reads alone. While
its owned backend and app are stopped, the driver records one zero-write
commit, with no application row, schema, log event or outbox change. The
manifest names this sync marker and all three segment receipts, timings and
screenshots. This is lifecycle evidence, not a substitute for the deeper
application assertions above; empty partitions and required-argument queries
remain explicitly limited evidence.

Round-one review hardening binds restart to the barrier sequence in both the
launch configuration and driver validation, so a pre-barrier tail cannot
stand in for reconnect progress. A successful segment's owned PID must still
exist at deliberate termination. Device state is reread after the render
pause before attesting counts and identity. `createApp` notifies the native
host only after publishing a client; the runner follows replacements and
cancels a closed client's in-flight sample without a failure receipt.

## 3. Acquisition on the same app

Run the full retained fixture on the actual client, identifying the offline
content required before measuring. Record first useful render, completed
durable acquisition, transferred rows/bytes, disk size and peak memory where
the host exposes it, then prove offline reopen. If peak memory cannot be
measured, name the absent metric; do not substitute JavaScript heap for all
native or wasm memory.

Preserve LLP 3000's physical-floor comparison only for equivalent fixture,
host and persistence semantics. Smaller reference-app timings and server
query latencies do not substitute for this measurement. These observations
also evaluate the residency proposal, without making it responsible for
native module correctness.

## 4. Deployment, restore and provider boundaries

Use an isolated disposable deployment with the existing release commands.
Exercise password session recovery, an asset read, schema adoption, a queued
job across process restart and the documented stopped backup/restore path.
Keep restored workers and old production clients fenced; do not reconnect a
prior outbox and claim recovery merely because fresh reads work. Test rollback
as source activation, not database rewind. Record the storage mode and the
precise crash class; this is not physical-power-loss qualification.

For one real push-provider adapter, select a test account/device and explicit
recipient before sending. The user-authorized implementation, publication
and benchmark program does not by itself identify a provider account or a
message recipient. Prepare the adapter and test-double crash cases first;
execute actual external delivery only with that account/recipient authorization.
No secret values belong in artifacts or LLPs.

Distinguish effect admission, provider acceptance and device receipt. Inject
the acceptance-before-local-settlement failure using a labeled test double;
then verify the real provider's supported deduplication/outcome behavior.
If a provider cannot deduplicate or resolve an uncertain result, record the
remaining ambiguity instead of claiming exactly-once external delivery.

## 5. Benchmark integration and availability

Before launching the next comparable Clique run, record package pins, builder
and grader identities, required simulator availability and the committed
probe set, or explicitly declare any protocol deviation. Preserve the frozen
instrument and perform native qualification as an explicit extension after
the cold build and independent grading. The prior builder prompt expressly
made simulator proof optional. Do not change that timed task silently. Do not quietly count a new
native proof as though previous runs used the same environment.

Append a compact coverage table to the existing report: scenario, host,
artifact link, result and limitation. A missing credential, simulator or
signing identity is an execution prerequisite, not a passing product test.
Continue independent browser/server work while that cell remains unavailable;
do not manufacture its outcome or replace it with a compilation check.

alone) lets the table's update or delete rule admit a viewer who cannot read the row; when that rule
refuses, a hidden row still answers `NOT_FOUND` and a readable one `E_RULE`. When a `NOT_FOUND`
case fails, `test` (and dev's refusal log) say whether the lookup's row was hidden by its read rule.

## An acceptance script over HTTP

Spawn the binary itself: `resolveBinary()` from `snapback4/bin-path` (file `dist/bin-path.js`) returns
the platform binary, so the child's PID is the server's (it runs the native verbs; `web-shell` is the
npm wrapper's, `node_modules/.bin/snapback4 web-shell dist`). The `node_modules/.bin/snapback4` wrapper also
works: it forwards `SIGTERM`/`SIGINT`, and the server stops when the wrapper dies, even by `SIGKILL`.
Never spawn `npx` (npm and a shell sit in between). `--port 0` takes a free port and `--memory` keeps no
store, so parallel runs never contend; a fixed port that is taken refuses at once (`E_ADDRINUSE`, naming
the holder). On a host that assigns you a port range, pass it: `--port 20100-20109` binds the first free
port in it (`E_ADDRINUSE` only when all are taken), so no port-scanning loop is needed. Readiness: stdout's first line is the URL and nothing else,
exactly `http://127.0.0.1:41234` (no trailing slash: request `url + "/q/cardsOn"`); host diagnostics go to stderr, so read stdout
alone, or, reading both streams together, match the stderr line `SNAPBACK4_READY verb=dev generation=1 url=http://127.0.0.1:41234 pid=4242`.
`GET /dev/status` names the server's `pid` (and `wrapperPid` through the wrapper). `snapback4 dev --help`
has the lifecycle.

Each scenario starts from state you know. Earlier steps change what later ones read (bob's list grows
when alice invites him), so assert every persona's baseline before the first write, and give each
scenario its own rows: `POST /dev/reset` before each one (below), distinct ids and owners per
scenario, or a fresh `dev --memory` per scenario (each start is an empty store). `/seed` is an upsert,
so reseeding alone does not remove rows a scenario added.

```js
// node scripts/acceptance.mjs
import { spawn } from "node:child_process";
import { once } from "node:events";
import { createInterface } from "node:readline";
import assert from "node:assert/strict";
import { resolveBinary } from "snapback4/bin-path";

// Stop a child for certain: SIGTERM, up to graceMs to exit, then SIGKILL. Safe after it exited.
async function stopChild(child, graceMs = 5000) {
  if (child && typeof child.url === "string" && typeof child.stop === "function") { await child.stop(); return; }
  if (!child || typeof child.kill !== "function" || typeof child.once !== "function" ||
      !(child.exitCode === null || Number.isInteger(child.exitCode)) ||
      !(child.signalCode === null || typeof child.signalCode === "string")) throw new TypeError("stopChild needs a ChildProcess or { url, stop }");
  if (child.exitCode !== null || child.signalCode !== null) return;
  const exited = once(child, "exit");
  child.kill("SIGTERM");
  const timer = setTimeout(() => child.kill("SIGKILL"), graceMs);
  await exited;
  clearTimeout(timer);
}
// `serve` returns the same handle; deploy the directory first.
const startServe = (directory, args = ["--port", "0"]) => startHost("serve", [directory, ...args]);
// `dev` on a free port with an empty store: its URL and stop(). Reuse it for every scenario and proof script.
const proofPort = process.env.PORT_LO && process.env.PORT_HI ? `${process.env.PORT_LO}-${process.env.PORT_HI}` : "0";
async function startDev(args = ["--port", proofPort, "--memory"]) { return startHost("dev", args); }
async function startHost(verb, args) {
  const child = spawn(resolveBinary(), [verb, ...args], { stdio: ["ignore", "pipe", "inherit"] });
  const died = once(child, "exit").then(([code]) => { throw new Error(`${verb} exited ${code} before its URL`); });
  died.catch(() => {}); // only the race below reports it
  try {
    const [line] = await Promise.race([once(createInterface({ input: child.stdout }), "line"), died]);
    return { url: line.trim(), stop: () => stopChild(child) };
  } catch (error) { await stopChild(child); throw error; }
}
// A refusal's code from either envelope (a query's {denied}, a mutation's {state: "failed", why}); undefined when answered.
const refusal = body => body.denied?.code ?? (body.state === "failed" ? body.why.code : undefined);

const dev = await startDev();
try {
  const post = async (path, body, persona) => (await fetch(dev.url + path, { method: "POST", body: JSON.stringify(body),
    headers: { "content-type": "application/json", ...(persona && { "x-snapback-persona": persona }) } })).json();
  assert.equal((await post("/seed", [{ table: "boards", row: { id: "b1", owner: "dev:alice", title: "Groceries" } }])).seeded, 1);
  assert.equal((await post("/q/cardsOn", { args: { board: "b1" } }, "alice")).data.length, 0); // baseline first
  assert.equal((await post("/m/addCard", { args: { board: "b1", text: "milk" } }, "alice")).state, "sent");
  assert.equal(refusal(await post("/m/addCard", { args: { board: "b1", text: "spam" } }, "bob")), "E_RULE");
  assert.equal(refusal(await post("/q/myBoard", { args: { board: "b1" } }, "bob")), "NOT_OWNER"); // a query's own require
  assert.equal((await post("/q/cardsOn", { args: { board: "b1" } }, "alice")).data.length, 1);
} finally { await dev.stop(); }
```

### Per-test isolation: `POST /dev/reset`

One dev serves the whole suite; `POST /dev/reset` before each test empties its store and seeds the
test's rows in the same transaction, in a few milliseconds:

```js
// Inside the script above, after `post` is defined:
const reset = async seed => {
  const answer = await post("/dev/reset", { seed });
  assert.equal(answer.reset, true, JSON.stringify(answer)); // a refused row answers {denied}; nothing was reset
  return answer;
};
for (const [name, scenario] of [["alice adds a card", async () => {
  assert.equal((await post("/m/addCard", { args: { board: "b1", text: "milk" } }, "alice")).state, "sent");
}]]) {
are reported and never invalidate valid product types. No store adoption, sync
or authority change is introduced.

Upgrade takes the old installed CLI's check snapshot before installation, then
invokes the newly installed CLI's check after pinning. Compiler path resolution runs in a fresh Node process
for each check: package-manager symlink replacements (pnpm) cannot reuse the
helper's cached old realpath after installation. It preserves the package
manager's dependency sections and ordinary recovery. A compiler refusal after a
successful install returns nonzero with a message distinguishing installation
from qualification; it never rolls back the packages silently. Module-only backends are projects too: all `snapback/*.q` files qualify through
the same loader; schema.q is not required. Without any Quarry source, upgrade
reports package pins only. Exact apps receive one rebuild pointer:
`bun exact.mjs snapback --rebuild` (HS owns the command and device handshake).
`guide changes` packages the release card; its version is bound to the compiler
version and its factual nullable-count examples are covered by the guide tests.
Nullable numeric aggregate writes offer `EXPR ?? 0` as an authored choice, not
auto-apply: hidden keys remain null, authorized missing count groups return zero.

The witness uses the declared persona independently of a guest-only sign-in
screen by selecting createApp's normal persona option under the explicit
witness flag. It still verifies the actual viewer before writing. Custom apps
retain their visible persona picker; a missing persona app/picker refuses. No persona
works against serve. Default starter examples use guest sign-in; `--as alice`
on a starter that declares alice also works.

A visible form proof is opt-in with `--field LABEL --value TEXT --button LABEL`.
It fills the labelled control, presses the labelled button and observes that
click's matching durable mutation through the registered app client. After filling it arms the exact labelled button, and accepts a mutation entered while
that button's trusted Chromium click remains active (through either client or app).
A form submit event is not click evidence. The default starter calls its shared
submit command from onClick (preventing the duplicate default form action); Enter
continues to use that same command. Short microtasks that remain inside the active dispatch may submit; work after
dispatch ends may not. Finish longer asynchronous validation before enabling
submit. A private witness-only options symbol follows unapproved App calls
through readiness, so a submit/other-context continuation cannot borrow the
restored enclosing click at Client entry. It is never serialized or a client
card field. Tagging reads each supported option once and retains both values (id and
waitSeconds), including inherited/non-enumerable getters, without changing the
caller object. This scoped proof never attributes background
or input autosave writes to a button. Before clicking it arms a fresh observer: an existing sent status or row cannot satisfy
this proof. It waits for terminal sent and asserts the configured args when
provided; failed or timed-out custody is nonzero. No second SDK mutation is
injected. The ordinary SDK-only witness remains available without these flags.
React Native web uses keyboard events (`pressSequentially`) to settle state;
buttons use their accessible labels, not testID. Registration is flag-gated and
cleanup restores its instrumentation. Starters label their field, render write
status above the list, and supply a favicon.

Port selection precedence is explicit --port/--web-port, explicit --port-range,
then the complete PORT_LO/PORT_HI environment pair, then existing defaults.
Ranges are validated before writing any files, and every selected port lies in
them. The client URL, backend npm launcher, frontend dev/preview launcher,
README and witness script share those selections. Later runtime URL overrides
remain supported; the init range is a scaffold input, not an authority rule.
HS owns the Vite Node declarations; DX2 adds no parallel type shim.

Expo's web client and README derive their device-versus-journal choice from the
same compiled sync detection. Its smoke helper uses the schema's password,
guest or persona sign-in (password only on prime, with a witness-supplied test
credential; it never puts a secret in source). The helper is inert in ordinary
launches and other segments and waits for restoration. The native device and
normal identity custody remain unchanged.

Ordinary durable React `run()` owns an onWrite observer before admission and
waits for sent/failed, independently of read subscribers and mounted effects.
Early settlement cannot be missed and concurrent calls match only their own
saved/minted intent id. The promise releases its observer on terminal response
or thrown admission. Offline it waits while the client lives; closing the client
ends its ability to collect a receipt, so callers keep the App alive until
settlement or use the explicit custody option and saved id. Existing
`{ waitSeconds: 0 }` returns pending custody immediately; no pending result is
relabelled sent. This amends the default hook boundary, not client.mutate,
outbox durability, authority or replay-result availability. `sentResult` and
`otherwiseAll` already exist; the recipe and tests cover null, replay and all
five read meanings. No client card word is added.

Wire id omission already landed in §8 and is re-tested with the documented
full envelopes. Guides add tested loop writes and projection identity, list
supported formats (handle and url; email remains application validation), spell
out fixed-period UTC epoch anchoring and periodEnd, and carry the asset upload
route. Aliases resolve optional param, mutation default and service seat.
Non-TTY reads print a complete card; explicit section/find still narrows it.
REL2 owns release lifecycle narration, DIAG owns general diagnostics and PAGE2
owns planner/rules language; DX2's nullable-write guidance changes no semantics.


Wave-10 review record: round 1 Muse READY; fresh Sol NOT READY with four
material counterexamples. All accepted: preserve complete wave-9 starter byte
variants in adapt, recognize module-only projects, bind form admission to the
actual labelled button's active click, and refuse frontend overflow before
writing. Add controls for unrelated background writes and highest-port ranges.
The no-sync Todos README and stale piped-output wording are corrected as well.


Round 2: Muse READY; fresh Sol identified a later requestSubmit inheriting the
saved click as a material false positive. Accepted: the bridge now recognizes
only entry in the trusted clicked button event, never standalone form submit.
The starter's shared submit command is entered from onClick and prevents the
duplicate browser action. A Chromium requestSubmit-after-broken-click control
refuses. Restored independent section/find and executable recipe tests removed
by the earlier piped-output test edit. No product behavior beyond this scoped
proof boundary changes in the final delta.


Round 3 found that Chromium can retain window.event through short microtasks,
and that App readiness could restore a containing click after an unapproved
8:**Revised:** 2026-10-13 (§10: REL2 wave-10 release lifecycle); 2026-10-13 (MACFIX: explicit pre-retention compatibility boundary and canonical deployment/worker paths); 2026-10-12 (§5.1: path-independent control socket; implemented; dual-family confirmation, captured lifetimes, native generations, hot activation and complete qualification)
34:The required Darwin schema and worker files passed alone with default TMPDIR
57:Release activation events live in the database transaction with activation revision, operation, release/previous/generation, timestamp and retained/restored summary. Prune records its item summary in that transaction. Existing releases.jsonl and current remain checked convenience copies; post-commit filesystem errors explicitly say activation succeeded. History is never edited or rewound. Rollback picks an earlier immutable source, not a historical data snapshot.
63:serve owns the database sidecar for its lifetime and publishes a Unix-domain release-control socket in the private per-user runtime directory (§5.1; directory 0700, socket 0600). Check peer credentials on both ends (Linux SO_PEERCRED, Darwin getpeereid), requiring the effective UID. Only the host filesystem owner has this capability; it is not an HTTP door, browser origin, bearer service seat or remote endpoint. Check filesystem objects before replacing a stale socket; never follow symlinks or replace a regular file. Use `.release-command` (a hardened private regular file, no-follow, single link) for the command flock and the deterministic runtime address (§5.1) for the control socket. lstat socket entries; only a same-owner socket may be removed while holding the exclusive store sidecar. Bound command JSON to 4096 bytes and response JSON to 64 KiB, with a 5-second input timeout and 120-second command response timeout. The trusted same-UID operator can directly edit source/database; malicious same-UID edits are outside the threat model, but ordinary source races are caught by a candidate content digest over source/native/worker files in the request and verified by the server. Numeric release id is the only source selector; never a caller path. Commands hold a separate private command flock while allocating/staging immutable release source. They acquire the store owner when stopped; E_STORE_BUSY with a running serve uses the local socket instead of opening another writer. Dev and an older/unreachable server continue to refuse with an exact stop/retry command.
65:snapback4 releases <dir> reads authoritative current release, activation revision, generation and durable events without adopting source, including while serve is live. This is the recovery command for an ambiguous result. A bounded command message identifies the owned database inode/canonical identity, candidate release id, expected current release and monotone activation revision, operation and explicit flags. The server compiles only its own immutable release directory, prepares native module/worker prerequisites before commit, then holds its existing engine writer guard for activation and in-memory publication. The server verifies both expected current release and activation revision inside the activation transaction. Increment activation revision on every successful release activation, including an equal-hash source change; A→B→A cannot satisfy an old command. Concurrent commands serialize on the command flock; out-of-band stale commands fail compare-and-swap. No request may choose arbitrary filesystem source. Compilation/validation failure leaves the previous engine, bound schema, native module, worker revision and reader factory unchanged.
67:A query already running in a pinned SQLite read transaction finishes on its old snapshot and reports that generation. A writer already executing finishes before activation obtains its engine guard. Queued HTTP operations are admitted under one release request guard covering signature lookup, argument decoding and execution. Activation acquires its exclusive side before taking the engine guard; admitted operations finish before activation, and later operations dispatch under the new generation. Internal detached readers still fence obsolete identities with E_GENERATION. Fresh requests after publication use the new engine; a stale pooled reader not yet pinned retries through the replacement factory. Prepare a runtime bundle containing bound programs, precompiled native module/metadata, worker root and validated named declarations; prepare the reader factory from the post-activation engine while its writer guard blocks new writers (reader opening failure after commit clears the old factory and uses existing lazy retry, never aborts a committed activation or advertises the old backend). Swap bound/generation/native under the common bound guard in the existing /schema guard order, retire pooled readers, rebind ephemeral state, invalidate change answers, wake sync/polls and update worker revision while holding the writer guard. The supervisor explicitly switches its immutable release project root and named declarations on revision, including addition/removal/replacement; externally supplied --worker stays an operator-owned entry. Worker preparation captures an immutable (root, entry, declarations/environment, supervisor revision) tuple. Under the engine guard it compares that captured revision to the current worker revision before minting credentials; stale preparation is discarded and rediscovered. Never relabel an old prepared source with the latest revision. Credentials bind that checked revision so later activation fences them even when spawn finishes later. Workers restart using the candidate packaged source and native continuation hashes keep the existing conservative parking policy. Long validation can pause writes; hot means no process restart, not an unbounded zero-pause latency promise.
69:The server continues an admitted activation even if the client hits its 120-second response timeout or closes the socket; failure to write the reply is not permission to roll back or exit the server. The database is authoritative at crash or lost socket reply. Timeout/EOF after sending a command reports an ambiguous result and instructs inspection of current and durable history; never silently resend as a new release or claim failure left the old release active. Before activation fsync required immutable source/native/worker files and their directories, then the published releases directory entry. The supported guarantee covers process crashes and filesystem/SQLite durability on hosts honoring fsync; no guarantee covers failing storage hardware. Restart attaches the database-selected release even if current's convenience copy is stale. After database commit, publication is infallible in-memory assignments and committed ephemeral rebind; fallible native compilation, declaration/environment/import validation happens before commit. Worker process spawn remains fallible after commit: failure reports committed activation with degraded worker availability and retries; it cannot undo a release or external effects. Filesystem-copy failures report the committed result.
140:Every changed execution activation including A→B→A gets a fresh monotone semantic generation, never reuses a release number as a generation. Execution-content identity includes the prepared native module digest as well as schema/programs. A changed native digest advances the existing semantic generation atomically with release activation, even when native declarations are unchanged; removing a module changes the digest too. Equal execution content retains generation unless a release activation changes the final active/dormant population (§9.1), which requires a new replica generation; source-only activations still advance activation revision. The digest is SHA-256 over the exact prepared wasm bytes; the no-module sentinel is the empty string persisted as release_native_digest. activation_hash binds schema/programs and that digest. Comparison is with the current activation, so A→B→A is fresh. Startup attaches the persisted execution identity and verifies the immutable release module matches its recorded digest. This uses the existing device protocol, not a separate native epoch. Rollback and forward use the same generation replacement and full partition reacquisition already used by adoption: /schema advertises the exact active shape, sync rejects old watermarks, devices clear obsolete base/index state and rebase or refuse their queued intents under the existing authority/operation contract. Store id remains unchanged; this is not a store replacement. No dormant rows or fields enter snapshots, partition logs, online answers or device stores. Never combine old-generation pages with a new backend. A late old read remains tagged old and cannot overwrite a newly observed generation. Simulate rollback/forward across a delayed sync page and audience changes; retained data changes server storage only, not device persistence formats.
142:Lock order: command flock is CLI-only; serving control never acquires it. Candidate filesystem/native/worker preparation precedes engine lock. Serving publication holds engine → bound → generation → native, and separately replaces readers while engine is held (matching existing checkout); no /schema handler takes engine while holding bound. Ephemeral rebind uses its existing committed infallible path, never a fallible post-commit store read. Update desired worker root before revision; worker thread releases root/bound guards before taking engine. Wake/seq/answers follow existing publish_engine while engine is held: these are short memory updates/nonblocking condition notifications, required before new writer admission; no socket/child wait occurs there. `releases` includes the existing worker status metadata so post-commit launch failure is visible as degraded availability. No SQLite lock is held while waiting for a child process or socket peer.
150:History/status is page-based with `releases <dir> [--after <event-id>]`; bounded pages return a continuation and inventory pages use stable item keys. Prune audit stores a complete item list in SQLite; bounded replies carry a continuation rather than truncate silently. Critical assertions include T(a,b) → absent T → T(a) → absent T → T(a,b), preserved b and latest updated a; identical parent id/value replacement invalidates dormant edges; stale revision after A→B→A and equal-hash activation refuses; activation-time parent expiry followed by identical-id replacement invalidates dormant references; rapid B→C activation with a barrier between B worker preparation and credential minting discards B conventional/named source; an old checked-out reader not yet pinned retries the new factory including native-only changes; a connected client holding native module A hot-adopts B with unchanged declarations, reacquires B backend/module and refuses a delayed A result; inject interruption before/after commit and during pointer-copy updates to distinguish no activation from committed activation with an ambiguous result.
156:Four initial revisions/review rounds are recorded verbatim under llp/reviews/. Muse returned READY on revision 4. Codex identified actual-deletion evidence, revision-bound worker preparation, and native-only content in semantic generation identity; all are explicit above. The coordinator subsequently authorized targeted confirmation, further review rounds and judgment calls, and commissioned implementation through tests and commits without another approval stop.
164:The host implements the private same-owner transport, prepared native/worker custody, atomic live engine publication, reader fencing, revision-bound worker preparation, named-worker mutation and database-authoritative crash recovery. Seven focused hot-activation tests and four transport tests pass; the complete host unit suite passes (393 tests plus four existing ignored). All 23 release CLI/client integration tests pass, including an already-connected native-only client, concurrent traffic, hot narrowing refusal and process death before/after commit. Restoring full tables installs the identity scan before read-through adoption. Historical recorded attribution (including nulls), parent bindings and captured deadlines survive restoration; only physically missing captures are filled, and effective deadlines recompute under the candidate. An older release may make a through reference mutable; restoration refuses if its current parent table/id differs from the retained binding. Both naming orders of joint parent/child retirement, an unswept expired attribution target, effective deadline visibility, partial capture deletion/reuse and older retargeting are regressions. A test-only publication barrier permits an owned subprocess kill after commit and before runtime/pointer/reply publication, followed by database-authoritative recovery and retained forward restoration. Named secret selections are validated in immutable preparation before commit. The device and simulator exercise delayed old snapshots and audiences on both stores. SERVER.md owns the lifecycle semantics, the evolution card supplies the schema bridge, and the OPS deploy card is a one-line pointer. Final full quality results and commits are recorded in WORKSTREAM-REPORT.md. No publication is claimed.
243:`rollback <dir> --to N --check` and `check --store <path> --release N` execute candidate adoption on a private SQLite snapshot created by VACUUM INTO through a read-only source connection. This is disk-backed, uses the exact adoption builder/validation, and never changes the live source, pointer, events or generation. SQLite supports VACUUM INTO on a read-only source; it acquires a coherent WAL read snapshot without the source writer lock. BUSY/disk failures refuse with retry advice, never fall back to copying sidecars. The temporary directory is owner-only and removed on success, refusal and ordinary unwinding. Process kill/crash may leave a private preview directory; operators can remove snapback4-release-preview-* leftovers when no preview uses them. The live format, including format 2, is never upgraded by preview. The snapshot may require disk/time proportional to the store; it provides a point-in-time plan, not a write freeze. The output enumerates the full candidate inventory and totals every narrowed population, not just the first sixteen status items; it includes any E_ADOPT refusal. Preview prepares the candidate native content identity but never starts workers or effects.
251:Buffer bounded request bytes before final release admission. A fair release request lease then spans each one-shot /q and /m's release-dependent lookup, typed argument decoding, execution and reply-identity capture. Release it before serialization/socket output, including every refusal/replay path. A scoped cleanup releases it on unwinding/early exits. Queued exclusive activation prevents new shared admissions, avoiding writer starvation. Release activation takes the exclusive lease across adoption and publication. Acquire it before engine/bound/readers locks. Sync and held polls retain their existing generation negotiation and wake behavior, so a long poll cannot block release publication. The guard admits concurrent readers; activation may pause new operations while it waits for current ones. Dev retains predecessor snapshot reading during adoption. Each engine JSON reply carries `generation`; custom application route bodies remain byte-owned by the application and carry the header. query, schema and sync envelopes that already specify their pinned generation retain that value; replay and generic refusals use the admitted request generation. Authentication, asset preparation/admission (including early refusals), durable actions/effects, service minting and ephemeral work share an engine-work accessor that stamps under the actual producer lock after buffered intake. Authentication enablement is rechecked against that same engine schema at credential collection/mint, including after KDF; successful ancillary credential checks and commit notifications do not relabel a captured result. Bound-only/raw metadata captures its own bundle identity. Durable actions stamp the engine that admits or collects after buffered intake, without holding a release lease during worker waits. Raw native/asset/following/application-route responses use the header, without modifying owned bytes. Stamp it while holding the engine that authorizes/collects the result, after body intake; a request-arrival stamp alone cannot label a later engine generation. The HTTP X-Snapback-Generation header carries the same value. Stream/asset replies use the header. Unknown operations return 404 denied E_OP; typed mutation argument decoding and range failures return 200 failed E_INPUT (with the request id when supplied); query argument failures and malformed JSON retain their 400 denied input envelope. No one-shot release operation exposes adoption loading images or E_GENERATION.
258:Muse (muse-spark-1.3-contributor, high) and a fresh GPT-6.1-sol (high) found the initial amendment NOT READY. Their verbatim records are under llp/reviews. Adopted: finite reference closure and rebuild ordering; ordinary hidden-id refusal; concrete bridge repair when both unique conflicts are dormant; VACUUM read-snapshot/error/crash semantics; precise latest/no-op/warning vocabulary; input buffering and reply lease release before socket I/O. GPT's nominal-reference objection is resolved by preserving LLP 3000.039's no-existence-check law. Its lifetime objections are resolved by separating initially dangling evidence, ignoring candidate-omitted edges, and retiring predecessor expiry before hiding. Round-two reviewers receive the revised amendment and implementation independently.
280:Round eleven: Muse READY; GPT found auth/guest minting under a new engine with the intake generation label. Producer capture is now shared across authentication and remaining worker/metadata paths, with intake-switch regressions. Explicit releases N/latest inspection fulfills the literal selector request in addition to the latest listing field.
```
